Nebex CEO: NASA wants a commercial space economy. Here’s how it can enable one
2026-11-01

Nebex CEO: NASA wants a commercial space economy. Here’s how it can enable one

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
In July, SpaceX stopped accepting new rideshare bookings beyond late 2028. In October, NASA released its request for proposals for commercial space stations to succeed the soon-to-be de-orbited International Space Station (ISS). Taken together, the ISS’s retirement and the squeeze on launch availability creates a real problem for many “new space” startups that built their venture business plans on continued cheap rides to orbit. While alternatives to SpaceX exist, they are limited and spotty: Rocket Lab’s Electron offers dedicated small-satellite launches, while ULA’s Vulcan and Blue Origin’s New Glenn serve larger payloads (although both are temporarily grounded: Vulcan since February and New Glenn since April). New competitors are advancing too: Isar Aerospace secured a launch contract with Planet, Stoke Space raised $1 billion and is targeting an initial launch in 2027, Rocket Lab’s larger Neutron rocket is expected to debut in 2027 as well, and Skyroot had a successful launch on its first attempt. But still, most, if not all, of these options remain unproven. Starship’s first orbital flight in September may eventually ease the overall launch bottleneck, but it’s not certain that the increased capacity will be made available to the third parties that are depending on it. For a startup space economy that scaled around frequent, inexpensive rideshare slots from one company, the challenge now is how soon the alternatives can deliver dependable access to orbit at comparable scale. While developing new rockets and replacing the ISS present different operational hurdles, both run into the same underlying market gap: building for space is extraordinarily capital-intensive, and the industry lacks a discovery mechanism to turn existing global demand into the committed orders and upfront capital needed to bring new capacity online. Global government demand is not the issue. According to Space Foundation, governments spent roughly $141 billion last year, and budgets continue to accelerate. The main problem is pretending a self-sustaining commercial market exists when space companies are relying on the U.S. government to be the anchor tenant, effectively asking the American taxpayer to subsidize their venture-backed businesses. In September, pushing back on a former NASA official who called for more agency-funded crew vehicles, NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman wrote: “You cannot simply slap the word ‘commercial’ on every problem or opportunity.” He was arguing that NASA can’t fill every gap in the market. I agree, and I’d go further: while global demand is there and is far outstripping available supply, the market itself needs to be nurtured. NASA has the procurement leverage to make that happen without footing the bill alone. I joined the space sector as an outsider from the tech and startup world in 2021, and over the next four years went on to close complex deals to send sovereign astronauts (from Italy, Sweden, Turkiye, India, Poland, Hungary, Saudi Arabia, and the UAE) to the International Space Station. I also closed deals with Fortune 100 companies to send everything from orbital compute and pharmaceutical research to in-space manufacturing payloads to orbit. This resulted in $1 billion in net new sovereign and commercial business, not tied to a U.S. government contract. Through that experience, I learned firsthand that building a true commercial space economy is possible but requires significantly more market infrastructure and business model innovation from both the private sector and the government to be successful. Isaacman’s stance makes one thing clear: government handouts won’t fix this, and neither will depending on a single launch provider. While NASA is not designed to create or regulate markets, it can structure the programs that shape them. In its recent request for proposals to replace the ISS, the agency’s evaluation criteria explicitly penalize bidders that rely on substantial amounts of taxpayer funds or experience prolonged negative cash flow, while rewarding outside commercial and sovereign revenue. Yet tight bid timelines and strict flight-heritage requirements for human spaceflight often leave primes with little choice but to rely on legacy aerospace suppliers. They end up paying steep markups upfront for hardware, while hoping international and commercial tenants materialize years later. To fix that disconnect, NASA doesn’t need a bigger budget; it needs to push for acquisition strategies that connect a station’s supply chain directly to its future customers and financing. First, the agency can streamline the supplier vetting process to incorporate suppliers from allied and partner nations from the get-go, cutting hardware and service costs immediately. More than just vendors, these companies and their governments are the very ecosystem partners—and commercial and sovereign tenants—that future stations will rely upon for revenue. Second, NASA should place a premium on binding, reciprocal commitments from those partners to use the stations once on orbit. Finally, since NASA’s milestone payouts are heavily back-loaded toward major technical events, the agency should consider partnering with market infrastructure providers who can underwrite those day-one supplier costs against committed customer revenues. This will spare builders from draining cash reserves or diluting their shareholders. Connecting global supply discovery, reciprocal demand, and access to credit in a single framework, whether to build commercial space stations or aggregate demand for emerging launchers, creates benefits for every party involved: primes can submit cheaper, more competitive bids, international partners gain a stake in the orbital economy, and U.S. taxpayers are spared from bankrolling the entire transition alone. If we want to ensure that the U.S. and its allies have a viable alternative that rivals the capabilities of our spacefaring competitors in the East, we will have to leverage the full force of the global market—and that means involving these commercial and international stakeholders from day one. The opinions expressed in Fortune.com commentary pieces are solely the views of their authors and do not necessarily reflect the opinions and beliefs of Fortune. Tejpaul Bhatia is the founder and CEO of Nebex, a platform that connects buyers, suppliers, and capital across complex transactions from demand to delivery. He is a space investor, executive, and entrepreneur. His career spans transformative product, platform, and business model innovation across startups and enterprises including Axiom Space, Google, Citi, and ESPN. Nebex is building an exchange for space procurement that connects space companies directly to sovereign buyers and capital, so Bhatia has a significant stake in the approach proposed here. He is also a large shareholder in Axiom Space, which is bidding in NASA’s competition to replace the ISS. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Voters usually decide one or two income or wealth tax measures a year. This midterm season, there are nine
2026-11-01

Voters usually decide one or two income or wealth tax measures a year. This midterm season, there are nine

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
California’s billionaire tax has gotten the headlines, and the billionaires paying to stop it make up a good share of them. They’ve poured tens of millions of dollars into defeating Prop 40, a one-time 5% levy on their net worth to help offset federal cuts to health spending. Google cofounder Sergey Brin and others are pushing efforts for Props 41 and 42, which would effectively stop Prop 40 were it to pass. But Prop 40 is one entry on a much longer list. Voters in six states will decide nine statewide measures on income or wealth taxes this year. From 2000 to 2025, voters decided 40 such measures—a pace of roughly one and a half a year—and they’ve been evenly split: 20 approved, 20 defeated. Lawmakers in these states haven’t been asking voters to raise taxes on the rich: most measures that would create or raise taxes were put there by citizen initiatives, created by activists and unions. This year, there are measures to increase taxes on the rich in California, Colorado, Washington, Massachusetts and Cook County, Illinois. California Gov. Gavin Newsom and Democratic gubernatorial candidate Xavier Becerra both oppose Prop 40, while the California Democratic Party endorsed it (Newsom has since backed Prop 42). In Colorado, Democratic Gov. Jared Polis opposes the graduated income tax, while in Washington, Democratic Gov. Bob Ferguson is leading the defense of the state’s new tax. The states voting on taxing the rich also hold a large share of corporate America. California has 56 Fortune 500 headquarters, Illinois 29, Massachusetts 15, Washington nine and Colorado seven. As of Forbes’s 2023 count: California had 186 billionaires, Illinois 23, Massachusetts 22, Washington 13 and Colorado 12. Meanwhile, Republican-leaning states are doing the opposite. This midterm’s measures include one in North Carolina, which would cut its constitutional cap on income tax rates in half, to 3.5%. One in Iowa would require a two-thirds legislative vote to raise income taxes, and another in Utah would require 60% voter approval for new or higher taxes. Missouri’s Republican-led Senate sent voters a plan to invest state money in the S&P 500 until the returns can replace taxes. “Over the last few years, we’ve seen a divergence in state income taxes and taxes on high earners,” Jared Walczak of the Tax Foundation told CNBC. Washington voters rejected income taxes in 10 ballot measures between 1934 and 2010. The last of them, in 2010, split the state’s richest families: Steve Ballmer and Jeff Bezos each gave $100,000 to oppose it, and Bill Gates Sr. gave $500,000 to support it. This year, lawmakers passed a millionaires tax themselves after a 25-hour floor debate. Now opponents have put a measure to counteract it. Amazon, Microsoft and Costco are all headquartered in Washington. Bezos has already sold his Seattle mansion and moved to Miami, while Ballmer, Gates and MacKenzie Scott remain among Washington’s billionaires. If the tax survives, it takes effect in 2028, with the first payments due in April 2029. In Colorado, Amendment 87 would replace the 4.4% flat tax with six brackets, from 3.7% to 8.4% on income over $1 million. Colorado would be the third state ever to switch from a flat to a progressive income tax, and it would do so over the objection of its own Democratic governor. In Massachusetts (which was the second state to switch), revenue from its surtax has exceeded projections, bringing in more than $3 billion in the 2025-2026 fiscal year. Now, voters will decide whether the proceeds should count toward the state’s revenue limit. Cook County’s question is advisory, so it won’t change any law, but it comes after Illinois House Speaker Emanuel “Chris” Welch dropped a statewide millionaires tax this year when Democrats couldn’t muster the supermajority needed to put it on the ballot. The state’s last attempt, in 2020, failed 55% to 45% after Gov. JB Pritzker spent $56 million supporting it and Citadel’s Ken Griffin spent nearly $54 million opposing it. An advisory vote in the county that includes Chicago lets supporters gauge support without risking another statewide loss. Pritzker is on the ballot again this year, running for reelection, and his race drew an unusual voter. Pope Leo XIV, the Chicago-born pontiff, became the first pope to vote in a U.S. election, mailing in a ballot that includes the governor’s race. Pritzker posted, “If the Pope can vote all the way from Vatican City, you can too!” The pope is registered in Will County, though, so the Cook County question isn’t on his ballot. Catherina covers markets, the economy, energy, tech, and AI. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Want to increase voter turnout? Start with the workplace
2026-11-01

Want to increase voter turnout? Start with the workplace

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Tom Florsheim Jr. is chairman and CEO of Weyco Group. Adam Goodman is president and CEO of Goodmans Interior Structures. Both are part of Leadership Now Project’s network of business leaders; the group is named in this piece. It’s hard to improve voter turnout. Just ask campaign and PAC leaders who spend hundreds of millions of dollars each election cycle trying to get people to vote—blanketing TV and social media platforms with paid advertising, and investing in canvassing, phone banks, and mailers. All this money barely moves the needle. Randomized trials suggest that even a well-executed canvass can only juice turnout by a point or two at most. New research has uncovered a much more powerful lever for driving turnout: America’s employers. Researchers linked LinkedIn employment records to voter files—studying 30 million workers at 377,000 employers. They found that a person’s workplace is decisive in whether or not they choose to vote. To account for employee selection bias, researchers at Michigan, Harvard, Columbia, and Maryland also tracked 8 million people who changed jobs. Their findings? Workers who moved to a higher-scoring employer became 3.5 percentage points more likely to vote. If they moved from an employer at the 25th percentile to one at the 75th, they became 5.8 percentage points more likely to vote. And the effects hold across political affiliations. These results point to a striking conclusion: employers have enormous influence over whether or not their employees vote. We know that people are powerfully influenced by the environments where they spend most of their time—and workplaces top the list. Employers set the tone for civic engagement. They can remind employees that elections are happening and that democratic participation matters. They also control formal levers that can prevent people from voting, like scheduling and leave policy for Election Day. Most corporate leaders don’t think very much about employee voting—and they likely have no idea where their companies fall on this distribution. We believe they have an obligation to find out and that they should make an effort to climb the rankings. At the companies we run, we’ve found out that driving voter turnout is a management problem with an easy solution. At Weyco, we have a simple communications calendar. Several weeks before an election, employees are encouraged to make a plan to vote. When early voting opens, they hear about it. They then receive a friendly reminder the week before Election Day, and again on the day itself. Employees who want to vote or serve as poll workers are encouraged to take paid leave to do it, and we arrange a ride for anyone who needs one. Weyco also brings voter registration assistance into the building. At Goodmans, we’ve partnered with Power the Polls to recruit employees to serve as poll workers. Employees who participate receive a small stipend and a paid day off. In both cases, these programs are simple to administer and cost little. Neither requires an outside consultant or a new department. Wisconsin Business Leaders for Democracy publishes a toolkit with a communications timeline and templates any company can adapt. Both of us have also worked alongside fellow business leaders to come up with plans through Leadership Now Project. For most employers, the cost is a few emails and some scheduling flexibility. The overall playbook is simple: Give people time to vote, remind them when to do so, and help connect them to their local election officials to find out how. Another important step: encourage anyone interested to work the polls. Nearly half of local election jurisdictions had difficulty recruiting enough workers for the 2024 general election, according to the U.S. Election Assistance Commission. This fall, Milwaukee, Kenosha, and Maricopa County are short, along with jurisdictions in Ohio, North Carolina, Nevada, and many more. Poll worker shortages lead to longer lines and more friction for voters. There is a long-running argument in corporate America about whether and when a CEO should speak on matters of politics. For many, it remains a challenging question. The good news: helping employees participate in democracy requires none of it. At no point does an employer need to ask any employee about their political preferences or about specific candidates. And it doesn’t require leaders to take a political position or risk ending up in a news cycle. Better still, the effects won’t expire in November. The research suggests that voting is a habit-forming activity: employees continue even after they leave us. Those employees, in turn, influence their households—family members of workers at higher-scoring employers vote more too. And the impact of employers is largest in primaries, which are the contests where additional turnout makes the biggest difference. Helping employees vote is the right thing to do, but it’s also good business. Higher levels of participation lead to greater trust in our democracy. That, in turn, reinforces the stability that lets businesses plan for the future, invest, and hire. Employers are already shaping whether or not people vote. Our experience is that with even minimal effort, they can use that power for good. Randi Weingarten: the fastest fix for the affordability crisis is a working-class tax cut
2026-11-01

Randi Weingarten: the fastest fix for the affordability crisis is a working-class tax cut

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Randi Weingarten is president of the 1.875 million-member AFT. The most effective way to solve America’s affordability crisis is to put cash back in people’s wallets. We can do that by electing a new Congress on Nov. 3 that is committed to passing the Working Americans’ Tax Cut Act. Despite what President Trump claims, higher costs for American families are no hoax. We’re all paying more to fill our gas tanks, feed our families, and keep a roof over our heads. The average family is paying $4,200 more for basic necessities since Trump returned to office. The Working Americans’ Tax Cut Act would provide direct relief to working- and middle-class Americans. The math is simple. The bill exempts the first $46,000 of income for individuals—and $92,000 for married couples—from federal income taxes entirely. And it provides a substantial tax cut to middle-class families. If you’re a new graduate making less than $46,000 at your new job, you’d pay no federal income tax. That’s life-changing for someone just starting out and trying to make it on their own. If you’re a nurse or teacher making $50,000, you’d receive a tax cut of approximately $2,800. And a family of four making $95,000 would receive a $6,000 tax cut. That’s not pocket change. It’s a significant economic boost that helps people afford the basic building blocks of a decent life and the relief would be widespread across our country. According to the Institute on Taxation and Economic Policy, the Working Americans’ Tax Cut Act would benefit 130 million Americans, including 25 million children. This tax cut wouldn’t add a cent to the national debt because it’s paid for by forcing rich Americans to pay their fair share. Millionaires and billionaires would pay a new tiered surcharge on their wages and other income such as capital gains and investments. The Yale Budget Lab estimates this would raise $1.46 trillion over 10 years. So not only does this get cash in the hands of people who most need it, it also restores fairness by creating a more progressive tax code and valuing work. It’s unconscionable for a hedge fund manager or a billionaire to pay less in federal taxes than a teacher or firefighter. The Working Americans’ Tax Cut Act can’t happen unless we elect a new Congress in November. Congressional Republicans have refused to even consider the bill and have already adjourned, abdicating their responsibility to help the people they represent. We know the priorities of the status quo Congress because we’ve lived with the consequences. This Congress has spent the last two years acquiescing to the costly demands of President Trump. The only meaningful legislation they passed was President Trump’s so-called Big Beautiful Bill, which gave $1.3 trillion in tax cuts to the wealthy by cutting healthcare, food assistance, jacking up student loan payments, and making life harder for American families. And some of the worst impacts of the bill don’t even go into effect until January. The President’s war with Iran has caused record prices at the pump and increased inflation. All told, Americans are paying on average 50% more for a gallon of gas since the Iran war started and heating oil is up 60%. Because of Trump’s Big Beautiful Bill, the average family will pay $4,000 more on their student loans and those who get their healthcare through the Affordable Care Act have seen their premiums rise by 50%. Grocery prices are now 4% higher than when President Trump took office and families have had to spend an additional $833 on housing costs. So yes, working- and middle-class Americans need a tax cut and they need it now. The Working Americans’ Tax Cut isn’t a panacea. We must still fight to make healthcare and college affordable, increase the minimum wage, ensure a secure retirement for all, and strengthen the right of every worker to join a union and negotiate a better future with their coworkers. This working- and middle-class tax cut, and the direct relief it provides, moves us closer to restoring the basic American bargain that if you work hard you should be able to provide a decent life for your family. But only if we elect majorities in Congress who commit to passing it. Fortune | FORTUNE
RAND and USC health researchers: Even big self-insured companies can’t rein in health costs
2026-11-01

RAND and USC health researchers: Even big self-insured companies can’t rein in health costs

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Christine Buttorff and Petra W. Rasmussen are policy researchers at RAND; Rasmussen is also a professor of policy analysis at the RAND School of Public Policy. Erin L. Duffy is a scholar at the University of Southern California’s Schaeffer Center for Health Policy and Economics and a professor at USC’s Price School of Public Policy. This open enrollment season, employers and workers alike are again confronting high cost increases for health coverage, with family premiums now averaging over $28,000 a year. Some small companies report spending more on health insurance than they make in profit. For the past decade, the federal government’s response to rising employer costs has been to improve price transparency—the theory being that so long as companies know the price of things, they can shop around or negotiate their way to lower costs. But disclosure is not enough, we were told during interviews with employers, benefits consultants, claims administrators, and legal and policy experts. Few outside the very largest employers can turn price information into leverage. This is true even when it is the employer who directly foots the medical bills. Companies with such “self-insured” plans cover two-thirds of the 165 million Americans who get insurance through their job. They should have the clearest line of sight into health costs, but they don’t. That’s because self-insured employers usually hire a claims administrator, known in the industry as a third-party administrator or a “TPA.” It’s the TPA that actually assembles a network of doctors and hospitals and processes the bills. The arrangement works well so long as the TPA’s incentives align with the employer’s, but often they don’t. For instance, many market experts we spoke with pointed to particular fees as evidence that saving employers money isn’t always TPAs’ first concern. One fee, known as a “shared savings fee,” was described by experts as drastically inflating employer costs. A legal expert highlighted the impact of a shared savings fee with a hypothetical example in which a medical provider sent a bill for $100,000, the TPA negotiated the price down to $1,500, and then charged the employer $30,000 for negotiating that discount. To the employer, this would be reported as a $31,500 total claim. “I don’t think they really tell you that $30,000 of that is a fee,” the expert said. Even if the employer does get a fee breakdown—as some recent regulations require in select cases—then what? National insurance carriers have bought up independent administrators and bundled specialty vendors into all-in-one packages, so there aren’t many places for employers to go. Switching TPAs also often means changing provider networks, which draws pushback from employees. There are fixes experts say are worth pursuing: contract terms that cap fees and require data access, transparency requirements with actual enforcement teeth, and clearer rules on whether administrators, not just employers, bear some fiduciary duty to spend health plan dollars wisely. But each of these avenues has limits. Enforcement resources tend to flow to the employers big enough to demand them. And even a well-informed employer can’t negotiate hard if there’s no credible alternative. A handful of self-insured employers have tried opting out of the current system entirely. Some contract directly with local hospital systems. Others have shifted to fully insured coverage or sent employees into the ACA market after providing them with a defined premium contribution to cover some of the costs of their coverage. These paths, however, require a geographically concentrated workforce, deep administrative capacity, or a stable individual marketplace. This year, Congress enacted striking new transparency rules for pharmaceutical benefit managers as part of the Consolidated Appropriations Act of 2026. These require extensive disclosure to employers and limit some PBM fee practices. But to really lower costs, the rules need to do more than mandate disclosure. They need enforcement that doesn’t assume every employer has the power, leverage, and resources needed to enforce its own rights. And they need to go beyond PBMs and reckon with an industry that has consolidated to the point where knowing the price doesn’t mean you can take your business elsewhere. Ireland joins European Lab for Learning and Intelligent Systems network

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Alongside Norway, it is Ireland’s first time being included in the pan-European network. Ireland has joined the European Laboratory for Learning and Intelligent Systems (ELLIS) network after officially launching ELLIS Unit Ireland. The new unit marks the first time Ireland has joined the network, as ELLIS welcomed other new units in Austria, France, Germany and fellow first-timer Norway. The network has 49 units across Europe and aims to propel the continent as a leader at the forefront of AI research. The units operate as localised hubs whereby researchers can work together, share ideas across a range of disciplines and carry out long-term research. The unit is part of Rinn AI, which is Ireland’s national centre for research and innovation in data science and AI and is funded by Research Ireland. Prof Noel O’Connor, Rinn AI’s director, will lead ELLIS Unit Ireland, which will focus on advancing fundamental AI research in core machine learning and related areas such as computer vision, natural language processing and information retrieval. The unit will also work closely with domain experts, policymakers and societal stakeholders, as a means of addressing real-world challenges. There will be a focus on connecting researchers across the country and training the next generation of ML and AI researchers. Commenting on Ireland’s inclusion to the network, O’Connor said: “Joining ELLIS is a significant moment for AI research in Ireland. Through Rinn AI, we have brought together researchers from every university in the country and ELLIS Unit Ireland now connects that community directly to Europe’s leading network in machine learning. “It opens new doors for collaboration, gives our researchers and students a platform on the European stage and helps ensure the AI we develop is shaped by the people it serves.” SiliconRepublic.com recently spoke with Prof Paul Townsend, a photonics specialist in the School of Physics at University College Cork, about his plans for the semiconductors research centre that sits within Research Ireland’s new Rinn Network. Silicon Republic
Spooky science: How your brain conjures up night-time scares
2026-10-30

Spooky science: How your brain conjures up night-time scares

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
University of Bristol’s Prof Michelle Spear looks at how your brain could be responsible for things that go bump in the night, not ghosts. A version of this article was originally published by The Conversation (CC BY-ND 4.0) It’s three in the morning. You’ve woken from a dead sleep because you’ve heard a thud in the darkness or a floorboard creaking downstairs. Or perhaps you’ve woken because you have the unmistakable sensation you’re not alone. During daylight, these phenomena might be dismissed as the heating, your neighbours or the sounds of an old house settling. But in the middle of the night, they can feel a tad more sinister. Your mind might immediately start reeling, jumping to the worst-case scenario – wondering if you’ve been broken into, or if you’re being haunted by some supernatural force. In reality, the explanation is far more simple. Things really do go bump in the night. But occasionally, the culprit is closer to home than we might imagine. Falling asleep is not the neurological equivalent of switching off a light. The brain moves gradually from wakefulness into sleep, accompanied by changes in sensory processing, muscle activity and awareness of the world around us. But these changes don’t always occur in perfect synchrony. So for brief periods, characteristics of sleep and wakefulness can overlap. The results can be surprisingly spooky. If you’ve ever been on the verge of sleep only to feel that you’ve fallen off a kerb, missed a step or dropped through the bed, you’ve probably experienced a hypnic jerk. Also known as a sleep start, this is a sudden involuntary contraction of one or more muscles as we move into the earliest stages of sleep. It’s an example of how the transition between wakefulness and sleep isn’t always a smooth one. Hypnagogic experiences can also involve vivid sounds, images or sensations. So as you’re on the edge of sleep, you may think you hear footsteps or whispers – or you may even think you see a figure in the room. Exploding head syndrome – perhaps the most alarming name for a harmless sleep condition – is another example of how the boundaries between sleep and wakefulness can become blurred. As someone is falling asleep, or occasionally waking, they suddenly perceive an enormous noise: an explosion, gunshot or slamming door – yet nothing in the outside world has made a sound. Its precise cause remains uncertain. But the phenomenon demonstrates something important: hearing a sound and a sound actually occurring are not quite the same thing. The conscious experience of hearing is constructed by the brain. So neural activity generated without an external sound can still produce an auditory experience that seems entirely real. The brain doesn’t even need to be asleep for this to happen. If you’ve ever thought you’ve heard someone call your name – only to turn around and find no one there – you’ve been tricked by your brain. Another possible explanation for the sounds we think we hear at night involves source monitoring. This is the brain’s ability to distinguish events generated internally from those coming from the outside world. Thoughts, memories and imagined speech are internally generated, yet normally retain the quality of being ours. But occasionally, that attribution goes awry – allowing something generated within the brain to feel as though it came from somewhere else. Around sleep, that boundary becomes particularly interesting. Another common – but frightening – experience that can happen at night is waking up with the sudden feeling that someone else is in the room. This experience is usually the result of sleep paralysis. During rapid eye movement (REM) sleep, when most of our vivid dreaming occurs, the brain temporarily suppresses activity in most muscles. This protective mechanism, known as REM atonia, prevents us from physically acting out our dreams. But during sleep paralysis, our waking awareness returns while this physical paralysis persists. You know where you are, but cannot move. Yet elements of dream perception can remain active. This is why you may have experienced the sensation that someone – or something – is in the room with you, despite clear sensory evidence of the contrary. One clue as to why this happens comes from experiments examining how the brain distinguishes itself from others. In one study, participants controlled a robotic device positioned in front of them, while a second robot, which was placed behind them, reproduced these movements by touching the participants’ backs. But when researchers introduced a slight delay between the movement and the touch, some participants began to feel as though somebody else was touching them. Some even sensed another person standing behind them. While this experiment doesn’t explain every felt presence that can happen during sleep paralysis, it does reveal something remarkable: the distinction between ourselves and others is something the brain has to construct. Usually, movement, touch and our sense of body position agree about what belongs to us. But if that relationship is disrupted, an internally generated sensation can begin to feel like it originated elsewhere. Now picture this happening in a dark bedroom, when you’re partly awake yet still on the edge of dreaming and unable to move. The sensation that somebody else is there becomes rather less mysterious. Perception is not a passive recording of the world. It combines incoming sensory information with expectation and previous experience to construct the most likely explanation. But at the boundary between sleep and waking, this process can become less reliable. We may hear sounds that were never made, experience fragments of dreams intruding our bedroom or interpret sensations arising from our own bodies as the presence of somebody else. Michelle Spear is a professor of anatomy at the University of Bristol. Her research focuses on the architecture of synovial joints, drawing on her experience in histopathology to examine clinical and functional aspects of tissue-based research. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Sign up for the Daily Brief, Silicon Republic’s digest of need-to-know sci-tech news. Silicon Republic
Rinn Semiconductors director’s vision for the sector in Ireland
2026-10-30

Rinn Semiconductors director’s vision for the sector in Ireland

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Prof Paul Townsend tells us about his plans for the semiconductors research centre that sits within Research Ireland’s new Rinn Network. Over the summer, seven new Research Ireland centres were launched to address key research areas deemed pertinent to the country’s scientific, medical, economic and technological development. The centres belong to a national research network called ‘Rinn’ and between them are set to receive €460m in Government investments over the next eight years, supporting 577 research positions, developing more than 800 PhDs and collaborating with 17 research-performing organisations. The seven Rinn research centres – dedicated to the fields of advanced therapies, AI, energy, medical devices, pharma and biopharma, quantum, and semiconductors – began their work in July, with each being led by directors who are experts in their sectors. Rinn Semiconductors is set to receive nearly €71m of Government funding overall and is headed by Prof Paul Townsend, a photonics specialist in the School of Physics at University College Cork (UCC) and with Tyndall National Institute who was born and grew up in the UK. “I studied physics at university, gaining a PhD from the University of Cambridge on the novel properties of polymers that behave as one-dimensional semiconductors,” he tells SiliconRepublic.com. His subsequent work in industry included time spent at British Telecom on quantum cryptography, the then-emerging concept of exploiting quantum physics to secure information. This work, he explains, was ancestral to “the quantum information and computing revolution that we frequently read about” today. “In 2003, I had the opportunity to move to Ireland with my family and colleagues to found a new group at UCC and Tyndall, working on optical fibre communication systems with funding from Science Foundation Ireland,” says Townsend. Tasked then with supporting the development of the wider photonics research ecosystem and engagement with industry in Ireland, this has since become the focus of his time working with Tyndall as its head of photonics research. “My ambition is to take this an exciting further step forward with Rinn Semiconductors,” says Townsend. The centre is set to focus on research and innovation in heterogeneously integrated semiconductor systems, and aims to lead Ireland’s response to the point at which traditional improvements in computer chips diminishes (the end of Moore’s Law scaling) by pioneering modular integrated circuit-based – or chiplet-based – semiconductor design and integration. Chiplet architecture is a method of using multiple smaller modules to build a single larger processor for a more powerful end result. The Rinn Semiconductors team will seek transformative advances in these technologies and to apply them in three main areas: future internet, sustainable energy and the environment, and digital healthcare. “Today, semiconductor chips touch nearly every aspect of our daily lives; they are in phones, computers and medical technology,” notes Townsend. “Almost any problem you can think of – societal or technological – requires the use of semiconductors to provide a solution. “This is also a technology where Ireland plays a leading role. We are home to a world-class semiconductor industry sector – with a 50-year operating history – that employs 20,000 people in more than 100 companies. “The national semiconductor strategy, Silicon Island, aims to build upon this to position Ireland internationally as a hub of semiconductor excellence and to contribute to the EU Chips Act goal of growing the EU’s proportion of global semiconductor manufacturing output.” Key elements of the Government’s Silicon Island strategy, he notes, include strengthening research capacity, supporting start-ups and spin-outs, and building a future-ready talent pipeline through education and skills programmes. “Rinn Semiconductors is extremely well-positioned to make major contributions to the achievement of these goals,” he says. Another “key objective” of the centre, according to Townsend, is “to foster deep collaboration across the seven universities and institutions involved” by leveraging the “world-class” semiconductor fabrication facilities and infrastructure at Tyndall. The centre plans for what it calls “radical collaboration” with and between Dublin City University, Munster Technological University, Trinity College Dublin, Tyndall, UCC, University of Galway and University of Limerick. This, it is hoped, will lead to “a cohesive ‘atom to systems’ value chain in Ireland, connecting everything from basic materials research through to complete technology solutions”, says Townsend. The Rinn Network, in his view, offers the opportunity to leverage coordinated research at scale, which is “critical” to the development of complex technologies such as semiconductors. “The significant scale of the industry collaborative research projects we have established in just the first two months of operation gives me great confidence that this vision can be realised.” As centre director, Townsend is responsible for ensuring that his programme is “suitably prioritised, innovative, impactful, internationally leading in its focus areas and, critically, delivers on its mission and goals”. He is also in charge of budgeting, and overseeing grant compliance and reporting. “In addition, I take the lead on stakeholder and external relations management, and act as an advocate for the centre and its mission,” he says. Another important part of his leadership role is “to ensure the centre is a beacon for attracting and developing talent at all levels from PhD students to senior principal investigators (PIs)” in order to “address the demand from industry for highly-skilled scientists and engineers, as well as to sustain the academic research ecosystem needed to supply these trainees and keep Ireland at the forefront of semiconductor research”. Apart from the development and implementation of the centre’s scientific and operational strategy, Townsend – whose personal research background is in optical fibre communications and sensing – gets some hands-on time at Rinn Semiconductors, too. “I am also an active PI in the centre, where my group focuses on developing new integrated semiconductor solutions for two main application areas: energy-efficient interconnect technologies for AI clusters in data centres, with the goal of constraining the overall power consumption of AI; and distributed acoustic sensing in optical fibre cables, where we target improved monitoring of undersea communication cables and offshore windfarm power cables to ensure resilience and protection.” Moving forward, Townsend says his Rinn centre is targeting progress in four main areas: research breakthroughs in semiconductor chiplet integration and packaging technologies; translation of these ideas into new manufacturable technologies and products; acting as a catalyst for talent development; and driving engaged research programmes with stakeholders for impact on policy and society. The latter, he hopes, could include “addressing the early stages of the talent supply problem by working with teachers and policymakers on how to encourage more school and university students, particularly women, to take up physics and engineering subjects that can lead to a career in the semiconductor industry”. Townsend notes, too, the potential of the overall network for fostering collaboration and shared progress across the seven cornerstone areas, and the prospect of creating “greater value and impact than could be achieved by a group of individual centres working in a less connected way”. “The wider Rinn Network plan adds the exciting possibility for centres to work together on complex problems that can only be addressed through an interdisciplinary approach. “I think the scale and ambition of the overall programme will also be a great attractor for external talent and a fantastic springboard for researchers already in Ireland to advance their careers. An especially important goal to him on an individual level, he adds, would be “the development of the next generation of exciting, talented researchers who will take Ireland on to even greater success in the future”. “I am very fortunate and proud to have had the opportunity to work in Ireland and to contribute at least somewhat to the development of the national research ecosystem. “On the personal side, my wife and I and our two now-grown-up daughters all live happily in and around Cork – and wouldn’t want to be anywhere else.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Soaring memory prices – driven up by the continued unprecedented demand for AI chips – has had a notable negative impact across the consumer devices market, including personal computers. According to data from Counterpoint Research, global PC shipments fell 21pc in Q3 compared to last year. The report also forecasts a 27pc year-over-year drop in sales for the upcoming quarter. “Looking ahead, we see the PC slowdown continuing at least through the first half of next year,” noted Intel chief financial officer David Zinsner. “We remain optimistic about the opportunities across our broader portfolio, and we will continue making disciplined investments in capacity and working capital to increase supply and support future growth.” Just days ago, Intel announced plans to purchase Israeli quantum company Classiq to create a full-stack quantum computing platform. Astrophysicist Jocelyn Bell Burnell awarded UCD Ulysses Medal
2026-10-30

Astrophysicist Jocelyn Bell Burnell awarded UCD Ulysses Medal

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Jocelyn Bell Burnell. Image: Chris Bellew, Fennell Photography The astrophysicist has a long legacy of scientific research, discovery and supporting the wider STEM community. Astrophysicist Jocelyn Bell Burnell has been awarded the UCD Ulysses Medal for her outstanding scientific achievements and ongoing commitment to developing the next generation of researchers. The Ulysses Medal is awarded to those who have contributed something of immense importance on a global scale. Previous recipients include author Margaret Atwood, former Irish President Mary McAleese and the ‘Godfather of AI’, Prof Geoffrey Hinton. Raised in Northern Ireland, Burnell previously described her early STEM education as challenging. Despite her assurance that she wanted to study the stars surrounding our planet, her gender precluded her from engaging with STEM subjects in school, as they were aimed primarily at young men. She persevered, becoming one of only three young girls taking science in her secondary school, where she topped the class in physics and then continued on to the University of Glasgow and then Cambridge, where she undertook a PhD. It was at Cambridge where she worked on the development of a radio space telescope designed to explore the furthest reaches of space and where she discovered a “scruff” on one of the charts she was analysing. This was the moment she identified what would become known as a pulsating radio star, or pulsar. Despite her work however, Burnell was notably excluded when the discovery was recognised by the Nobel Prize in Physics in 1974. The prize was instead awarded to her PhD supervisor, Antony Hewish and a fellow Cambridge professor, Martin Ryle. Her exclusion stands as a prime example of how the achievements of women were so often disregarded in favour of their male co-workers and supervisors. In 2018, she donated £2.3m to start the Bell Burnell Graduate Scholarship Fund, a fund supporting students coming from underrepresented backgrounds. The money was part of the Special Breakthrough Prize in Fundamental Physics that she had earned for her previous discovery and five decades of leadership in the field. Prof Lorraine Hanlon , a full professor of astronomy at UCD, said Burnell had been “peerless in how she has deployed her brilliance, transforming her groundbreaking discovery into lifelong advocacy for curiosity-driven research, equity and global science diplomacy. “The achievements of all the Bell Burnell scholars stand as living proof of Dame Jocelyn’s enduring legacy in recognising and empowering the next generation of scientific leaders.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Innocent People Are Being Accused of Shoplifting by Faulty AI-Facial Recognition Systems at an Alarming Rate

Innocent People Are Being Accused of Shoplifting by Faulty AI-Facial Recognition Systems at an Alarming Rate

Futurism | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
From New York City to the streets of Tokyo, creepy AI-facial recognition security systems are quickly becoming a common sight across the world. Unfortunately for many, the tech is nowhere near ready for the responsibility of live use as the troubling rise of false-positive matches makes clear. In the United Kingdom, AI surveillance systems in retail stores have led to a surge in customers being wrongly accused of shoplifting. TechRadar highlighted a number of recent cases throughout the country, where shoppers say false accusations have left them shaken, with a feeling that their privacy had been horribly violated. As one woman identified as Mary told the publication, her husband has been socially “isolated” after a quick jaunt into a Home Bargains store for vitamins went awry. When the stores’ system flagged the anonymous man as a shoplifter, the couple was forced to leave by management, seemingly without any chance to refute the accusation. “I was scared to go into a shop,” the man told TechRadar. Another man was said to be “shaking from head to toe” following his wife’s removal from a Budgens chain store for the crime of stealing “loads of meat” days before — an accusation complicated by the fact that they hadn’t set foot in the store for months. In these and other cases, the publication reports a dizzying labyrinth of corporate bureaucracy and vague insinuations that leave innocent people unable to access their local businesses, and with little recourse to clear their name. In the UK, individuals can file Subject Access Requests, or SARs, which are like US-Freedom of Information Act requests but for private companies. Though the avenue technically exists for those wrongly accused of shoplifting, it’s a massive undertaking just for the hope of clearing your name — and requires handing over even more private information to private companies. As head of advocacy at civil liberty group Big Brother Watch Jack Coulson told TechRadar, it’s “the stuff of Kafkaesque nightmares to be trapped in a maze of bureaucracy and forced to hand over even more personal data just to be told the accusation.” More on facial recognition: Innocent Man Freed After Spending Over 50 Days in Jail Due to Horribly Inaccurate AI Facial Recognition Tech

Fox News Takes Massive Ratings Hit As It Triples Down on Pro-Data Center Coverage
2026-11-01

Fox News Takes Massive Ratings Hit As It Triples Down on Pro-Data Center Coverage

Futurism | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
It’s obvious by now that data centers are political kryptonite. Across the US, bipartisan backlash to data center projects has upended city councils, while billionaire developers and industry-aligned political candidates are being routed by grassroots opposition. But beyond those running for or occupying political office, the backlash is also taking a toll on news networks viewed as pushing pro-data center propaganda. Case-in-point is Fox News, which has tailed president Donald Trump in embracing the AI industry’s narrative on data centers — an editorial decision which happens to coincides with viewers fleeing the network in droves. According to a new analysis by Media Matters for America, coverage spinning data centers in a positive light shot up by a staggering 420 percent across Fox News over the past two months. On August 18, a memo issued by the National Republican Senatorial Committee went out to party insiders and top corporate executives, warning that opposition to data centers presented a major challenge to the party’s victory in the upcoming US midterm elections. As Media Matters notes, Fox News quickly fell in line, increasing the total number of pro-data center claims to 203 between August 19 and September 2, compared to just 39 from August 5 through August 18. Bizarre claims of “far-left activists” and Chinese government propaganda likewise proliferated, teeing up Republican faithfuls to blame a boogeyman for the rise in anti-data center rhetoric. Surprisingly, the tactic appears to have backfired, as even the network’s most devoted acolytes reacted with disgust. “Just in case there were any dummies who thought Fox News cares what voters want,” one disaffected viewer wrote in the YouTube comments under an extremely sunny Fox and Friends segment on data centers. “They’ll always protect corporate interests over conservative interests and (perhaps) even over protecting Trump in certain cases.” “For the first time I’m going to say, FOX is GREEDY,” another angry viewer commented. “For people who like to say they believe in God, [they] sure are failing on following his word.” Recent reporting by Status found that viewership of Fox News is in freefall following the shift — plummeting down 27 percent across the month of October to an average of 1.1 million total viewers. That data comes from media tracking company Nielsen, which also noted that viewership across the months of July, August, and September shriveled by 16 percent compared to the previous three months. Viewers in the 24 to 54 age range — a crucial demographic — tumbled 24 percent over the period. While there are likely a litany of other reasons for the shift, it’s nonetheless a striking correlation that coincides with a broader falling out with Trump as president. American voters overwhelmingly disprove of his handling of the economy, not to mention the extremely costly war on Iran. With a crucial midterm election coming up, it remains to be seen how the backlash against the right’s embrace of third rail issues like data centers will cost Republicans at the polls. A US Satellite Caught China’s Top-Secret Spaceplane on Camera
2026-11-01

A US Satellite Caught China’s Top-Secret Spaceplane on Camera

Futurism | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
For years now, space trackers have watched as China’s top-secret spaceplane, dubbed Shenlong, has been circling the Earth, releasing mysterious objects as it goes. But getting an actual look at the experimental craft has proven near impossible — until now. As Ars Technica reports, a satellite owned by commercial operator Vantor (formerly Maxar) snapped a picture of the plane in 2024, which has finally been released two years later. The photo shows a vehicle with two slim wings and what appears to be a docked satellite featuring two large solar arrays. The overall design is closely reminiscent of the US military’s own spaceplane, the Space Force’s top-secret X-37B. The image was released as part of the Pentagon’s recently unclassified Defense Intelligence Agency assessment on space-based threats. Behold: The US Defense Intelligence Agency has released a report on challenges to security in space, including an NEI image of China’s spaceplane from Maxar, taken in 2024. pic.twitter.com/F3msSwoFph However, beyond giving us a rare, first-hand look, the report itself offers little in terms of what the spaceplane is actually doing in orbit. China has been extremely secretive about the purpose of its four spaceplane missions over the last six years. The country’s space agency has previously claimed that Shenlong will “pave the way for more convenient and affordable round-trip methods for the peaceful use of space in the future.” Beyond releasing mysterious objects while in orbit, experts suggest the spaceplane has also been used to capture other objects, possibly using robotic arms. Meanwhile, the Space Force’s X-37B, which was developed by Boeing, has spent thousands of days circling the planet over the last 16 years. Its most recent mission launched in August 2025, well over a year ago. In 2024, the plane slammed its brakes in orbit during a risky “aerobraking maneuver” designed to lower itself from a “highly elliptical orbit.” Beyond getting a closer look at China’s equivalent, the Pentagon warned in its latest report that the Chinese military has “multiple ground-based laser weapons with varying power levels to disrupt, degrade, or damage satellites.” “By the late 2020s, China may field space-based directed energy systems and higher-powered ground-based directed energy systems capable of threatening satellites in higher orbits,” the report reads. Polymarket Just Can’t Stop Getting Hacked Over and Over Again

Futurism | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Polymarket’s soothsaying powers apparently didn’t foresee the platform getting hacked — again. The controversial prediction market lost millions of dollars of its users’ money after hackers breached its defenses on the international version of its website on Thursday, forcing the company to temporarily suspend trading. According to the Wall Street Journal, some $2.6 million was stolen from around 700 traders. Polymarket hasn’t confirmed how many users were affected by the hack, but told Wired that it will refund the lost money. Notably, this isn’t the first major breach the company has suffered this year. In June, Polymarket admitted that a sophisticated phishing attack allowed hackers to inject malicious code into its website and siphon over $3.1 million from around a dozen Polymarket user wallets. Last year, there was also an alleged attempt to manipulate the final outcome of a bet on Ukraine’s mineral rights. The cause of this latest security incident, according to the WSJ, is a flaw in DebugBear, a third-party website performance monitoring and debugging tool that Polymarket uses. Its website, at press time, is inaccessible and returns a DNS error. On social media, Debugbear said that it had been “impacted by an ongoing security incident” and urged customers to remove its tool’s embed code from their websites. Polymarket, for its part, said on X that it had fixed an issue related to a third-party vendor and promised the traders who were impacted that they would be “made whole.” Beyond repeatedly getting hacked, Polymarket has been rife with scams and insider trading. Last month, the WSJ reported that scammers were making accounts with stolen debit cards on its US platform and using them to place bets to effectively launder the money. The fraud was so prevalent that at one point a company that was processing debit card transactions for Polymarket flagged over 80 percent of its transactions as fraudulent. In all, the launderers attempted to make out with nearly $10 million. More on prediction markets: “Prediction” Markets Were Comically Wrong About the Brazilian Election

Sam Altman to Infuriated Mathematicians: The Math Slop Won’t Stop
2026-10-31

Sam Altman to Infuriated Mathematicians: The Math Slop Won’t Stop

Futurism | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
OpenAI Sam Altman says sorry, mathematicians: the onslaught of AI math slop will continue. The ChatGPT maker sparked an existential crisis in the field by releasing hundreds of AI-generated solutions to math problems that haven’t been solved by humans, and sparked further controversy over how it chose to release its solutions and communicate with mathematicians. But this doesn’t seem to bother Altman all that much. In an interview on the Cheeky Pint podcast on Thursday, he acknowledged the disruption that OpenAI was causing to mathematicians, but said that the work must continue — for the good of humankind, he argued. “I think, on net, the world deserves to understand how the universe works,” Altman said the podcast appearance, as spotted by The Verge. “But I do have empathy for the method.” According to the outlet’s reporting, it’s the first time that Altman has directly acknowledged his company’s impact on mathematicians. OpenAI’s explosive foray into math started when it claimed to have found a solution to the famous Navier-Stokes problem by using 10,000 AI agents that worked 88 hours straight. A mathematician accused OpenAI of unfairly piggy backing off his unreleased research and threatening to ruin his career when he said he told them he would go public with his accusations. Prominent names in the field, meanwhile, began to worry about the potential new paradigm that OpenAI had potentially introduced: wealthy AI companies could now jump on someone else’s ongoing research and beat them to the finish line by throwing ungodly amounts of AI computing power at the problem, and then take credit for it; OpenAI had spent around $10 million just to come up with its Navier-Stokes solution. But that was just the beginning. Soon, it released hundreds of more AI-generated mathematical proofs, a move that was blasted by top mathematicians and even a math advisory group that OpenAI had been consulting with. Critics note how none of the papers have been vetted or reviewed, not least of all because the natural language proofs are borderline incomprehensible. It will take years for mathematicians to unpack all the AI-generated proofs OpenAI has dumped on their laps, while OpenAI, in the here and now, gets to use them as purported proof of their technology being revolutionary. On the podcast, Altman admitted that “we did let it get it out of control for one week on math,” but justified the endeavor because of the importance of what they were finding. “I feel unequivocal on this point: Humanity deserves fundamental knowledge, and we should not be sitting on a major fundamental science discovery,” he said. “The world deserves that.” More on AI: Pope Leo Comes Out Swinging Against AI Personhood

Medical institutions throughout the US are under siege, as a rising cohort of venture capitalists and risky biotech startups move in to exploit the Trump administration’s hands-off approach to health regulation. Chief among them is Palantir cofounder and billionaire venture capitalist Joe Lonsdale. The billionaire’s new startup, Safer Human Medicine, plans to sell picks and shovels to the new generation of experimental medical companies — in other words, macaque monkeys. As Forbes reports, Lonsdale’s venture capital firm 8VC has invested $20 million in Safer Human Medicine, a company which is working to breed some 30,000 macaques destined for medical experiments at the hands of America’s freshman bio-tech startups. During his previous role as COO of research animal supplier Envigo, a dog-breeding lab in Virginia was ordered to surrender over 4,000 beagles after federal officials found widespread violations of the Animal Welfare and Clean Water acts. Envigo’s parent company, Inotiv Inc., later pleaded guilty, agreeing to pay $35 million in fines. In a letter addressed to residents of Decatur County, Georgia — the planned site of a contested Safer Human facility — Harkness explained that in his eyes, the ends justify the means when it comes to animal research. Far from being unethical, Harkness wrote that what’s actually “immoral” is to “allow humans and animals to suffer from disease and illness.” Concluding, Harkness wrote that “the use of animals in human and veterinary research is viewed by those involved as a scientifically justifiable means to an end that must be preserved to ensure that humans and animals don’t have to needlessly endure disease and suffering.” More on animal welfare: Palmer Luckey Says He’s Breeding Genetically Enhanced Dogs for the Military

Data centers are now a midterm issue, even in Brooklyn
2026-11-01

Data centers are now a midterm issue, even in Brooklyn

TechCrunch | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE. On Thursday evening, I slipped on my neon yellow heels and a floral mini dress. It’s New York, so I can wear whatever I want to a midterm election event full of media types, techies, policy advocates, and, well, does anyone else even exist at these things? I walked into the dimly blue-lit Brooklyn warehouse and was surprised to see the room already filling up. Newsreel, the media company founded by former Forbes and Newsweek reporter Jack Brewster, hosted the event, and the lineup was heavy-hitting. Kenneth Schlenker, co-founder of the focus app Opal, sat down for a fireside chat about “the state of attention,” and Aryan Banjan, who built the campaign management software Barza, gave a midterm forecast. I, meanwhile, was moderating a panel on data centers. Brewster said the goal was for people to “put their phones away and talk to each other about the election, five days out.” As the evening progressed, however, one topic consumed nearly every conversation: AI, data centers, and the backlash against the growing technocracy. Lately, as a tech reporter, I’ve noticed a growing chasm between tech and everyone else. I spend most of my days talking to people who see the benefits of AI, who are so ambitious in building it and so optimistic that it will succeed that, when I talk to people beyond the tech world, I forget how ugly it all seems to them. Outside the tech bubble, data centers and AI have become such an intense ballot-box issue that they could sway some midterm races on November 3. A recent report from Data Center Watch found that almost $200 billion in data center projects were delayed, stalled, or blocked in the first half of 2026 due to political and community opposition. Meanwhile, states like Pennsylvania, Georgia, Texas, New York, and Virginia are considering more guardrails and transparency around data centers’ rapid development. Hundreds of new data centers are planned in Virginia alone, a state already nicknamed the “data center capital of the world.” A fireside chat with New York City Council member Chi Ossé, just before my panel, set the tone. AI came up near the end; Ossé said he doesn’t really use it. To me, though, he didn’t seem to be for or against it. He mostly emphasized that he would like to see tech executives held more accountable and trillionaires like Elon Musk taxed more. He described himself as a democratic socialist, but a “law and order” one. When CEOs talk about a technology that could deplete 10% of human civilization, he said, “they should be called to testify in front of Congress.” My panel was up next. I moderated a discussion with Maia Woluchem, a technology and human rights researcher, and Hilary Flint, an environmental and public health advocate, about data centers. As I took the stage, I decided to get a better read on the crowd. I asked, “Who here is pro-AI data center?” and was promptly booed. Our conversation covered a range of topics and emotions. People are upset that big tech companies appear to be cutting secret deals with local officials to build billion-dollar data centers. In some cases, zoning rules make it unclear what is being built until it’s too late to protest. At the same time, people are struggling with the cost of living and doing everything they can to hold onto what’s left of the American Dream, while tech leaders talk about AI replacing workers, or even humanity itself. Residents feel they have lost control over what happens in their communities, and they worry about the environmental and health risks. The panel agreed that data centers are the physical embodiment of people’s fears and anger toward the tech class and the rapid pace of technology. “What is a life worth?” Flint said when I brought up the benefits that some communities have seen from data center build-outs. People still protest, she said, because they “value their lives a little bit more than a temporary check.” The panel after mine featured NewsNation host Batya Ungar-Sargon; political strategist Morris Katz, who worked on Zohran Mamdani’s campaign; and Vox’s Astead Herndon. They were supposed to be talking about the future of the Democratic Party. But once again, the conversation shifted to data centers. Ungar-Sargon noted that some labor unions support data centers because of the jobs they promise. (Reports show that data centers create many jobs initially, but those jobs are not permanent once construction ends.) “There used to be a lot of pride in a community in the Rust Belt about very ugly, enormous buildings in their communities because everybody worked there,” she said, nodding to an earlier comment I’d made about data centers being ugly. “And they knew that building represented prosperity for themselves, stability for their children, and a future and a piece of the American dream.” Herndon noted that Vivek Ramaswamy was booed for making a similar point in Ohio, a Rust Belt state. “I just want to reinforce the lack of consent happening,” he said. “Because that’s true among Republicans in those places and I’ve watched it.” Katz was also skeptical. “It’s the same people who have taken so many union jobs away,” he said. “The same institutional powers that have robbed labor and working people of jobs and profit. Now coming back to essentially finish the job and take whatever scraps remain.” Along the warehouse walls, I noticed sticky notes answering posted questions, including “Will we ever be able to get Big Money out of politics?” “God, I hope so,” one note said. People rushed the bar when the panel ended, and techno music began to blast. They broke into small groups, where AI and data centers were once again the topic, this time in more private conversations. I asked Brewster if he was surprised by how much of the conversation focused on AI and data centers. “It came up in every conversation on stage, and it was the second most common topic in the questions people sent us when they RSVP’d,” he said, adding that it was happening even in New York, where data centers aren’t very visible. “AI has become an election issue for regular voters,” he said. “In rural communities, the backlash is about feeling replaced. In a place like Brooklyn, it’s more about who gets rich from it.” Before leaving, I made the rounds, asking people to name the craziest thing they’d seen in tech this year. Two tech founders mentioned the AI assistant startup Instinct, while a non-tech person named Sam Altman Another non-tech person brought up the rise of deepfakes and AI art. AI art, she said, is starting to signal cheapness. That may be why luxury brands are going the other way: Prada’s holiday campaign features hand-drawn illustrations, and Hermès has been spotlighting artists’ handmade work on Instagram. On my way out, I spoke to a software engineer who said the craziest thing she’d seen this year was a person who painted their nails orange for Halloween and was asked if it was a tribute to the Anthropic IPO. I admitted that I, too, had once worn a burnt orange dress and briefly thought of myself as the Claude logo. She later told me data centers are popping up in her Southern California hometown, too. Hearing that, I thought for the first time about how I’d feel if one popped up in mine. She grew up in a Chinese immigrant community, where neighbors are putting signs in their yards showing dragons choking a data center. The signs have since been translated into other languages, she said. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence. Judge allows NAACP lawsuit over xAI data center pollution to move forward
2026-10-31

Judge allows NAACP lawsuit over xAI data center pollution to move forward

TechCrunch | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE. A federal judge denied a motion by xAI seeking to dismiss an NAACP lawsuit accusing the SpaceX-owned company of failing to obtain permits needed to operate the gas turbines powering its Colossus data center outside Memphis. Suing on behalf of the NAACP, the Southern Environmental Law Center said the AI company had “installed and operated at least 35 combustion turbines and other sources of air pollution at the Colossus site without ever obtaining the necessary preconstruction or operating air permits.” It also accused xAI of violating the Clean Air Act. xAI sought to have the lawsuit dismissed, claiming that the turbines are exempt from permitting requirements because they’re mobile. The company also argued that the plaintiffs did not have the standing to sue. “Because the plaintiffs have statutory standing under the Clean Air Act, because the combustion turbines are not ‘mobile sources’ under Mississippi’s [State Implementation Plan], and because the citizen suit provision does not violate Article II of the Constitution or the private nondelegation doctrine, the plaintiffs’ claims will proceed,” Judge Debra M. Brown wrote in her decision. Focusing on xAI’s claim that these kinds of citizen lawsuits are unconstitutional, Judge Brown wrote, “Because citizen suit plaintiffs sue on their own behalf, in their own name, and seek penalties that redress harm to themselves, they are not exercising the federal government’s executive power.” TechCrunch has reached out to SpaceX for comment. In a statement, the NAACP’s director of environmental and climate justice Abre’ Connor described this as “another important victory in our effort to hold xAI accountable.” (A similar motion to dismiss from the Department of Justice was denied earlier this month) “The courts have repeatedly rejected attempts by xAI and the DOJ to dismiss the NAACP’s Clean Air Act case, affirming our right to pursue justice for communities impacted by pollution,” Connor said. “Our position has been clear: xAI cannot operate gas turbines without the required permits.” The NAACP lawsuit is moving forward as public opinion seems to have turned sharply against data centers, with New York recently passing a moratorium on new data centers and other state and local governments considering similar measures. The backlash also prompted Amazon and Microsoft to promise to stop using NDAs in their dealings with government agencies, and it could be a significant factor in the imminent U.S. midterm elections.
