The One Real Danger of Donald Trump’s Mary Peltola Threats
2026-10-31

The One Real Danger of Donald Trump’s Mary Peltola Threats

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Election mayhem season is upon us and it’s best to be prepared. In just the past week, Trump officials have sued to potentially block the certification of a Senate race in Alaska. They’ve threatened local California election officials with jail time. They have certainly implied that the outcome in a Senate race in Minnesota can’t be trusted unless the Republican wins. On this week’s Amicus podcast, Mother Jones’ national voting rights correspondent, Ari Berman, joined Dahlia to explain what we already know in terms of the attempts to throw sand in the gears of voting, and how all that is informing him about what to look out for this coming week. Their conversation, edited and condensed for clarity, follows. Dahlia Lithwick: When this Alaska lawsuit first hit, it did not pass the laugh test, right? Ari Berman: I mean, it was straight out of the Four Seasons Total Landscaping form of election law, in terms of how ridiculous it was. When I first started covering voting rights, the efforts by Republicans were to make it harder to vote before the election. Then what Trump did in 2020 was, he tried to throw out results after the election. Now, they’re trying to throw out results before the election. When we say that’s unprecedented, it really is. I can’t recall a single lawsuit like this that basically says, “If the candidate we like loses, you can’t certify it.” That’s basically what it said. I mean, it talked about some legal principles, but it was basically just a political document that said, “There’s two Dan Sullivans, and voters are evidently too stupid to figure out which one is which, even though one is on the ballot clearly identified as incumbent senator.” So they’re now asking a federal court to set aside the election if the Dan Sullivan they like doesn’t win, which I think has no chance of actually succeeding. But of course, this lawsuit’s not aimed at succeeding. It’s aimed at laying the groundwork to challenge the election after the fact. So now we really do have to talk about the skullduggery on the Democrat side, because we got a New York Times story this week about that other Dan Sullivan and how this all came to be. I want to give my cautions, because there is some context to be had. One, this ballot-stacking game is hardly new in American politics. And maybe more importantly, while it’s kind of gross, it’s certainly legal, and as you said, the ballot clearly names the incumbent. Nevertheless, skullduggery is skullduggery. Do you want to tell us what happened this week and whether and why it matters? The New York Times published a big piece. I would argue it was a slightly overheated and overhyped piece, in terms of how they presented it. The Times basically reported that the campaign, or operatives affiliated with the campaign, of Mary Peltola essentially recruited the other Dan Sullivan into the race to siphon votes away from Dan Sullivan, the incumbent senator of Alaska. To me, that seems like a political problem, not a legal problem. I don’t see how a court would change how they view this, because the Alaska Supreme Court already dealt with this issue. That’s the most important thing to know about this whole Alaska situation. This was already dealt with under state law by the Alaska Supreme Court. Officials in Alaska already tried to remove the other Dan Sullivan from the ballot, and the Alaska Supreme Court overruled them. So I can’t see how anything will change. Trump is basically saying on Truth Social that Peltola should not be able to run, not just that the other Dan Sullivan shouldn’t be able to run, but Peltola herself shouldn’t be able to run. The thing is, as you know, Dahlia, you can’t disqualify candidates via Truth Social. There’s a process here, and this process has already played out. To me, this feels like a political problem for Republicans in Alaska, and not a legal problem, even though the governor is saying they’re going to investigate, I don’t know what’s going to come of that. I do find it a little ironic that the Republican Party—which encouraged the hacking of a presidential candidate’s email, stole a Supreme Court seat, gerrymandered mid-decade, has done all sorts of things from Roger Stone to Lee Atwater to what Trump has done in terms of weaponizing the entire federal government, trying to overturn the 2020 election—is now saying this is election interference. They’re not exactly coming from, like, the high moral ground here. Right. It is that asymmetry that’s always a fork in our eye. When we go low, we freak out, and when they go low, we are just like, “Oh, well, that’s what they do.” So the asymmetry never fails to trip us up. And now the president has not just called for the Democratic nominee to be disqualified, but also criminally prosecuted, because he never wastes an opportunity to waste our time. Apparently in his view, this is a crime by the nominee. It just goes back to “lock her up.” It goes back to how he’s using his Justice Department as basically the revenge part of his government. They’re going after James Comey. They’re going after whoever he doesn’t like. Cassidy Hutchinson, even though they failed to actually indict her. I think it’s a lot of threats. I’m not concerned about that. What I am concerned about is this affecting election certification in Alaska. I think that’s the thing we should pay attention to. Because of ranked choice voting in Alaska, they don’t even tabulate the results of ranked choice voting until 14 days after the election, meaning it’s going to take a while to count the votes in Alaska. There’s going to be a ton of disinformation during that time, particularly if the Alaska Senate race is the seat that determines control of the U.S. Senate, which is entirely possible given how close it is. I think you’re going to see a lot of pressure not to certify the election brought to bear on Republican officials in Alaska. That’s where they’re going to rerun the 2020 playbook. Even though courts have very clearly said election certification is ministerial, not optional. That is one of the really big things we now know from 2020. You can’t just not certify an election because you don’t like who won the election. That said, they’re going to lean very heavily to not certify the election, and that’s why I would be more concerned, for example, about a situation like Alaska than California. Because in California, they have no power whatsoever to force the state to do anything. In Alaska, they could at least lean on the governor and the lieutenant governor to try not to certify. Then it would go to court; it might very well go back to the Supreme Court. I would imagine the election would end up being certified, but there is some uncertainty here in terms of how the process could break down, and obviously, the litigation by the Justice Department and then, more to the point, all the things the president has done to increase the chance that they’ll try to challenge the results of that election Just because they haven’t succeeded, and they’re deplorably bad at it, does that mean we shouldn’t worry, or doesn’t it matter how bad you are at it. If you want to unsettle election results, even doing it badly can have catastrophic results? It would be incredibly naive not to worry, but I think it’s worth noting that they incited an insurrection, but they didn’t succeed in overturning the election, which was their ultimate goal. And it’s a lot harder to overturn a midterm than a presidential election because there are so many different elections. It’s so decentralized. We’re not talking about five or six swing states here. We’re talking about House and Senate and governor’s races all across the country. Now, could it come down to one Senate race in a place like Alaska where they can try to manufacture some kind of dispute? Absolutely it could. But in terms of some of the hypotheticals—like not seating Democrats in the House, I mean, that would be very, very difficult to do. Just procedurally, it’s hard to do. It’s also hard to imagine it being within one or two seats. Nobody knows, but Democrats could win 25–35 seats in the House. It’s unlikely they’re going to win by one seat, based on how the polling is looking. And not just the polling, but look at where Trump is campaigning, where they’re spending money, all of those things. So the closer the election is, the better chance they have of contesting it. But I think the good news is, after 2020, there’s a lot we know about the system, and as I said earlier, certification is mandatory, not optional. The Justice Department can’t block certification of the election. They would have to convince someone not to do so. I think it’s very unlikely they will be able to convince a court not to do so, because there’s so much settled law from 2020 and after that you have to certify the election results. Absent a legitimate dispute about the election, a Bush v. Gore or a Franken versus Norm Coleman type scenario, we’re talking about a few hundred votes that are reasonably disputed. Absent that, the fact that you don’t like a candidate’s name, for example, that’s not going to lead to a lack of certification. In terms of leaning on local officials, that’s where I would be most concerned. Where do the vulnerabilities lie? There are new Trump-aligned people in power at the county level, and at the state level. Election deniers have taken over some of these positions. So that’s where I would be concerned that someone at the local or county level would try to block certification, and then it would have to go back to the courts, and the courts would ultimately say, “You have to certify the election.” I’m pretty confident of that. But again, that’s where the chaos and the uncertainty comes into the process. So the Alaska thing to me was a signal that they’re not going to be legally successful in overturning the election, but they’re going to try. There was a question before of whether they would even try. I guessed they would, but we hadn’t seen the evidence of that until now. Harmeet Dhillon was on 60 Minutes saying they’d only challenge it in crazy circumstances, and then they filed two lawsuits in the same week that are basically designed to block certification. So we now know they’re going for it, which shouldn’t be surprising. But going for it is different than winning. To me, this is more of a Hail Mary than it is like they’re on the goal line about to kick the game-winning field goal. So many people have done work behind the scenes now to prepare. A lot of people were caught off guard in 2020. They didn’t really think Trump was going to go through with it. They saw the hair dye falling down Rudy Giuliani’s face, and they just laughed it off and never thought it would get to an insurrection. Now people take this seriously, and the amount of work that’s been done behind the scenes and the amount of work that’s been done to Trump-proof the system and protect election officials makes me think they’re going to try some of this stuff, but it’s going to be very hard for them to actually pull it off, particularly in a midterm environment. Are people really going to storm the Capitol over a House race in Kentucky or a Senate race in Alaska? I mean, I guess it’s conceivable, but I just have a hard time seeing it happen. I think at the end of the day, rather than going all the way to Jan. 3, Trump is probably going to be really pissed off, blame a lot of different people, and go golfing. There’s a Likely Scenario for Election Night. It’s Scary for Democrats. It’s Scarier for Democracy.
2026-10-31

There’s a Likely Scenario for Election Night. It’s Scary for Democrats. It’s Scarier for Democracy.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
The Surge is your weekly political sanity check: What matters, what might, and what’s just political theater. Sign up here to get it in your inbox. Welcome to this weekend’s edition of the Surge, a politics newsletter whose job is not to make predictions in battleground Senate races and refuses to do so. (Unless asked, just one time and without much pushing at all, in a live show like the one we hosted on Thursday.) In this last edition before the election, we don’t have any big, sweepy analysis to make on the State of the Elections—that’s coming in a separate piece we’ve written for Monday—but here’s the long and short of it: Polls are very good for Democrats. Polls could be wrong. Polls could be right? Instead, we look at a few of the more discrete developments in the past week. The Michigan Democratic Senate primary drama between Abdul El-Sayed and Haley Stevens, one of the summer’s most watched programs, is back with a Halloween special. And hey, Barack Obama? You can come and take these cheeseburger spring rolls from our cold, dead hands. One note of housekeeping: The Surge will be doing a sleep-deprived live show for Slate Plus subscribers at noon ET the day after the election, analyzing results and assigning blame for the things we got wrong to other people. Now let’s turn our attention to the ongoing foofaraw in Alaska. The New York Times this week published a story detailing what everyone has long expected but hadn’t proved: that Democratic operatives associated with the Mary Peltola campaign and in Washington were behind getting a second “Dan Sullivan” on the Alaska Senate ballot to sow confusion among voters. Both D.C. groups and the Peltola campaign denied involvement to the Times, but c’mon. No doubt this is a trick designed to confuse Sullivan voters and get some of them to select the wrong Sullivan on the ranked choice ballot, without listing Sen. Sullivan as their second choice. The Alaska Supreme Court did a decent job, though, of sorting this out. While it didn’t find reason to toss Decoy Sullivan off the ballot—at the end of the day, eligible candidates are allowed to run for office—it did get Sen. Sullivan to be listed as the Republican incumbent on the ballot. The Democrats behind this aren’t as clever as they think they are. If they didn’t want to be caught, they should have pursued a spoiler strategy that didn’t involve picking a random guy with the same name: It was an instant giveaway that this wasn’t on the level. And the modern compulsion to text cheeky little jokes alluding to the scheme, once again, claims new victims. In terms of the remedy for this, though, everyone needs to get a grip. What Democrats did was recruit a spoiler candidate. That’s not new, and it wasn’t the only such effort to recruit spoiler candidates in this very race this year. The system then worked, with Alaska courts clarifying the ballot, Sen. Sullivan using the spoiler as an argument against Peltola in the campaign, and the media uncovering what had happened. If Alaska voters are so disgusted by what Peltola and Democrats did, they should vote against her as punishment. There’s a straightforward political remedy here. But that’s not the direction this is headed. The Department of Justice, which had already sued to block certification of the race should Sen. Sullivan lose reelection, is now issuing a flurry of subpoenas. Alaska’s governor has launched his own probe looking into civil or criminal penalties and is refusing to say whether he would certify Peltola as the winner. House Oversight Committee Chairman James Comer has started an investigation too. Trump, of course, has called for Peltola to be prosecuted. And if Peltola is certified as the winner and sent to Washington, he could urge Senate Republicans to object to her swearing-in, setting up a genuine shitstorm that the Surge would prefer not to cover. We do not believe that the remedy for Democrats recruiting a spoiler candidate should be for the Senate to start exercising discretion on which election winners it chooses to seat. We hate to sound like Mr. Rogers here, but: Let’s hope for a clean winner, one way or another. Because the Trump administration’s preferred path in a muddled result isn’t democracy-enhancing. When we visited Michigan before the Democratic Senate primary this summer, then-candidate Haley Stevens unequivocally told us that despite the nastiness of the primary, she would “absolutely” support Abdul El-Sayed if he became the nominee. She graciously followed through on that, at first, after losing the primary in a closer-than-expected result. Since then, she’s enjoyed some time off, some TMZ appearances, interviews here and there. She’s planning a wedding. What she hadn’t really said much about until this week, though, was the Senate race. And now she’s saying an awful lot. In an appearance on a CNN panel last week, she would say only that she was “still on the team” with El-Sayed (without mentioning his name), while offering a bevy of gentle criticisms about his campaigning tactics and observing that GOP candidate Mike Rogers is outhustling him in Detroit. Later in the week, it came out that her fiancé was supporting Rogers. Then, Stevens did another primetime interview with CNN, where she mentioned that she still supports “Abdul” but again lined it with criticism of his ads and observed that they might be making voters feel unsafe. What’s going on here? We don’t know—some combination of still being uncomfortable with El-Sayed’s Israel–Palestine politics, spending a lot of time with a fiancé who loathes El-Sayed, and a bit of sour grapes from the primary. It does appear that South Carolina Rep. Jim Clyburn, who endorsed Stevens in the primary, has come to play peacemaker and get the two ex-(?)rivals to appear together over the weekend. Stevens has continued to press El-Sayed, though, to more fully denounce a left-wing super PAC ad that was criticized as antisemitic. Stevens’ interventions this week won’t soon be forgotten by progressives. The big question posed by El-Sayed’s candidacy was whether someone from the Bernie wing of the Democratic Party could win a statewide Senate race in a true purple state. His polling has been better this week. But for most of the cycle, he’s been underperforming more-moderate candidates in other battleground states and substantially underperforming Michigan’s Democratic gubernatorial candidate. Now, win or lose—but especially lose!—the progressive movement will blame the underperformance on Democratic establishment figures for undercutting him. And the first name out of their mouths will be Stevens’. At a rally last weekend, former President Barack Obama joked that Trump’s “got the White House looking like Saddam Hussein opened a Cheesecake Factory.” Life has been incredible ever since. While this joke is not offensive to anyone, many feigned offense, with the interpretation being that Obama, ever the professorial elitist, was looking down on the good folks of Real America who enjoy the Cheesecake Factory. Stunts were pulled. The administration sent Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent—a near-billionaire who likely hasn’t dined at a chain restaurant since before he and George Soros shorted the British pound in the early ’90s—to pretend to enjoy eating lunch at the Cheesecake Factory. “I had a great meal, always have a great meal, and I would just tell the Martha’s Vineyard set to give it a try,” the finance tycoon told the press outside the restaurant. The White House X account released an A.I. slop video of Trump waving to the assembled masses outside a Cheesecake Factory. The Wall Street Journal editorial board published a column titled “Obama Goes Socialist, Scorns Cheesecake” (subhead: “He throws in with Florida’s Angie Nixon and condescends to Middle America”). For our younger Surge readers who may not have been politically aware during the Obama presidency: Every day was like this, for eight years. In retrospect: How charming. We’d analogize this to the moment in Trump’s 2024 campaign when a comedian joked, at a late-campaign rally, that Puerto Rico was “a floating island of garbage.” Now, that joke did actually offend plenty of people, while Obama’s joke offended zero people. But Democrats played it well beyond its worth as they tried to make something happen in the last week of a campaign they were going to lose. As you are all well aware, it did not prevent Democrats from losing that election. And—GUESS WE’RE GOING TO WRITE THE FOLLOWING WORDS—CheesecakeFactoryGate will not be the thing that prevents Democrats from doing well this year, if there is such a thing. • One key moment in Susan Collins’ 2020 reelection campaign was an endorsement ad featuring local broadcasting legend Bill Green. Green was a household name, and previously apolitical, so his endorsement carried weight. So we weren’t surprised to see Collins close with another, almost identically filmed ad featuring Green six years later. But the message Green delivers this time around is more pointed. In the ad, Green says that he knows Democratic nominee Troy Jackson, a logger by trade, but that “we’re not talking about buying a cord of wood here.” He adds that “Troy doesn’t have the tools he needs to be effective” in the U.S. Senate. That line allowed the Jackson campaign to respond that Collins’ team was condescending. Not as condescending as a joke involving the Cheesecake Factory, mind you. But nothing is. • If there’s a substantial polling error underestimating Republicans, this Washington Post write-up of its poll, which shows Democrat Adam Hamilton leading Sen. Roger Marshall in Kansas by 9 points, is a great explanation of why. Excited Democratic voters are sitting by their phones, waiting for pollsters to call, while pollsters are having a difficult time getting in touch with MAGA voters and divining a representative sample. The counter to that would be that with Trump off the ballot, maybe these voters aren’t answering polls because they’re less likely to vote, so the two effects net out to an extent. We’ll see. We’ll see!!! • We haven’t gotten a lot of public polls showing how Ohio gubernatorial candidate Vivek Ramaswamy’s grotesque ad treating the wife of an alleged rapist as a reliable narrator is playing in the state. But according to outlets that have seen private GOP polling, it has hurt Ramaswamy pretty badly. Such a shame. Couldn’t have happened to a nicer guy! The ad is no longer running. • After the Supreme Court’s Callais decision, which gutted protections for predominantly minority congressional districts, Tennessee Republicans quickly nuked the Democratic district in Memphis. But the race to represent what remains of Tennessee’s 9th District is drawing some attention and outside support for the Republican candidate, Brent Taylor, from Trump’s super PAC. This week, the Nashville Scene reported that Taylor “has sent unsolicited nude photos as recently as 2021.” Taylor, while not specifically confirming or denying the allegations, alluded to “youthful indiscretions” from his past that he’s worked through with his wife. Here’s the thing about those “youthful indiscretions,” though: Brent Taylor is 58 years old, and 2021 was five years ago. • Not to be a downer (totally to be a downer), but if Republicans lose both chambers of Congress next week because they’re yoked to an incredibly unpopular president, how does he respond? How does he change the subject? The answer may be right there in front of us: a resumption of the war in Iran. We’ll see you next week, treasured reader. There will be much to discuss. The Trump administration has sued to potentially block certification of a Senate race in Alaska, threatened California election officials with jail time, and challenged the voter rolls in 30 states. Trump’s Iran War Strategy: Out of the Frying Pan, Into the Fire
2026-10-30

Trump’s Iran War Strategy: Out of the Frying Pan, Into the Fire

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. If Republicans get thumped in next week’s midterms, Donald Trump’s war in Iran will be a big reason why. Many voters see the 8-month-old, $47 billion–and–counting conflict as a glaring violation of the president’s campaign pledge not to start new wars—one that has sent gas prices spiraling higher to boot. Yet regardless of just how much the elections end up looking like a rejection of Trump’s military adventurism, there are mounting signs that the president plans to escalate the fighting shortly after polls close on Tuesday. Let’s read the tea leaves. They’re everywhere. As the Wall Street Journal reported this week, U.S. officials have told America’s allies in the Gulf to brace for “a short, intense military campaign” after the elections. Per the New York Times, the Pentagon has drawn up plans for new strikes targeting Iran’s energy infrastructure, missile batteries, drone arsenal, and nuclear facilities. The U.S. is shipping more weapons to the region and preemptively evacuating embassies and military bases that Iran could target for retaliation. More U.S. warships are en route, carrying thousands of Marines. Iran and the Gulf states caught in the crossfire, meanwhile, are frenziedly stocking up on missiles and interceptors as if anticipating a return to hostilities. The president has publicly pledged not to strike Iran before the midterms, but he has also hinted that he has plans to do so once voting wraps. This month, Trump suggested that the U.S. would win the war “right after the election” and said, “We may do another little Epic Fury,” referencing the code name for the joint U.S.-Israeli strikes that started it. It doesn’t seem very Trumplike to show restraint. Why is he waiting until after the midterms, anyway? Carl Von Clausewitz once famously argued (and I’m both paraphrasing and translating here, since the dude spoke German) that war is the continuation of politics by other means. As the midterms have neared, though, this particular war has been more like the inverse: Domestic politics are dictating Trump’s approach to the fighting. Instead of greenlighting new attacks on Tehran, Trump has tasked the U.S. Navy with helping oil tankers transit the embattled Strait of Hormuz in a bid to hold down gas prices and save GOP seats. Iran, meanwhile, has been doing everything it can to keep prices high, upping attacks on tankers and having its proxies target Saudi Arabian oil refineries. After the vote, though, those incentives could flip. Unconstrained by Republicans nervous about losing their seats, Trump might escalate the fighting to try to force Iran to capitulate. So could the next attack be worse than what’s come before? Some Democratic lawmakers seem to think so. Chris Murphy of Connecticut, a member of the Senate Foreign Relations Committee who has been privy to classified battle plans, recently described the administration’s postelection strategy as “insane.” Without giving details, Murphy hinted that Trump could target civilian infrastructure in ways that would “lead to devastating escalation and the likely destruction of our commitment to the rule of law.” Um, yikes. What could that mean for the conflict? It depends on who you believe. According to the Atlantic, some Trump confidantes think U.S. sanctions have sent Iran’s hardline leadership “close to the edge,” and that another round of attacks “would push them over.” Other analysts think an escalation will just embolden Iran, which has already endured thousands of U.S. strikes. Well, keep in mind that elections can have consequences. If Democrats retake Congress, they could muster enough votes to block funding for the war. If Israeli opposition parties put aside their differences after this week’s election and topple Benjamin Netanyahu as prime minister, Trump could lose his biggest partner in the fight. And if the war keeps dragging on, it could become a millstone for Vice President J.D. Vance—or whoever becomes the next Republican presidential nominee—in the next election, too. They don’t call them forever wars for nothing. Without minimizing the scariness of war, it’s time for a hard pivot. Spooky season culminates tomorrow, and my colleagues have put together a frightfully good list of what to brew, read, and dress up as to get your weekend off to the right start: Creepy cocktails: Double, double toil and trouble! Plenty of people are brewing themed cocktails to celebrate Halloween, but too often these drinks wind up as tricks instead of treats. Courtney Iseman breaks down why gimmicky cocktails go wrong—and gives some recipe ideas for making your spooky spirits a smashing success. Icelandic Dracula: Shortly after Bram Stoker’s Dracula was published, a translation of the novel appeared in Iceland, with some very noticeable differences from the original. Where did Nordic Dracula come from? Who wrote this version? And did Stoker really give it his blessing? Tune in to this spooky episode of Decoder Ring for “a seasonally appropriate literary mystery—one full of doppelgängers, supervillains, plot twists, and vampires who just refuse to die.” Spirit Halloween: Have you ever wondered what it’s like to work at the seasonal costume superstore Spirit Halloween? Luke Winkie spoke with a 20-year-old assistant manager at one, and it turns out that it’s as bloodcurdling as it seems. Giant skeletons: Home Depot’s 12-foot-tall skeletons are some of the most coveted—and noticeable—Halloween decorations around. They first went viral in 2020, and they have been a hit since, towering over yards across America in all kinds of inventive tableaux. Heather Schwedel spoke with a Home Depot merchant to get the inside scoop on how these giant skeletons came to be. Enjoy the weekend! I’ll see you back in your inbox on Monday, with some special Slatest programming as we await the midterms. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
The Truth About the Alaska Dan Sullivan Shenanigans
2026-10-30

The Truth About the Alaska Dan Sullivan Shenanigans

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Earlier this week, the New York Times published a splashy feature detailing how allies of Mary Peltola, the Democratic nominee in this year’s Senate race in Alaska, worked to “stack” the ballot with candidates who could help her defeat the Republican incumbent, Dan Sullivan. As a result of these efforts, when Alaskans go to the polls next week, appearing alongside Peltola and Sullivan on the ballot will be one Daniel J. Sullivan, a retired teacher who voted for Peltola in previous elections—and who, according to the Times, was recruited by Democratic-operative types to play the spoiler role. The Times’ implicit framing of Daniel J. Sullivan’s candidacy as an election-swinging dirty trick requires a few caveats. First and most important, Alaska uses ranked choice voting, which means that Sen. Sullivan supporters confused by their two options can simply vote for both. Second, Peltola beat Sen. Sullivan by 8 points in the August primary, and Daniel J. Sullivan picked up 2.5 percent of the vote—significant, sure, but nowhere close to Peltola’s margin of victory. Third, Alaska officials have taken great pains to distinguish the two Sullivans on the ballot, displaying their names differently and designating Dan Sullivan as the incumbent. Also, just speaking practically here, if you believed that the presence in a tightly contested Senate race of a person with the same name as a vulnerable incumbent in a tough year for Republicans was a complete coincidence, I have it on good authority that the bridge to nowhere is getting built after all, and I would sell it to you for a favorable price. None of these facts have prevented President Donald Trump and his fellow Republicans from getting very angry about this stunt. Before the Times story was published, Justice Department officials, at the president’s direction, had filed a lawsuit seeking to block state officials from certifying the results if Peltola beats Dan Sullivan by less than Daniel J. Sullivan’s share of the vote. (Again, thanks to ranked choice voting, this scenario is extremely farfetched, but stay with me.) After the Times story, Trump went much further, calling for criminal charges against Peltola and the “FAKE Dan Sullivan.” He also demanded that both of them be removed from the ballot, something that would give Republicans nervous about losing the Senate one fewer seat to worry about. Sure enough, on Wednesday, Alaska Gov. Mike Dunleavy, a Republican, announced that state officials were looking at “whether this scheme violated Alaska’s laws.” The DOJ, which had an eye on the race already, issued new subpoenas to Democrats linked to the non-senator Sullivan, whose candidacy Attorney General Todd Blanche described as “cheating” and “fraud.” As is true of all breathless invocations of voter fraud by Republican politicians, however, Blanche’s claims do not hold up to scrutiny. The reality he is glossing over is that, as you would probably expect, there have already been legal challenges to Daniel J. Sullivan’s candidacy, based in part on some of the details in the Times story. In June, though, an Alaska court determined that under state law, he has the same right to run for office as Sen. Sullivan or anyone else. What Peltola’s campaign (allegedly) did might be partisan skulduggery, but it is probably not illegal, and no one is getting kicked off the ballot or going to prison over it. This case started back in May, when Daniel J. Sullivan filed to run for Senate as a Republican, despite never having previously been affiliated with the party. After the state GOP complained, the Alaska Division of Elections determined that under state law, Daniel J. Sullivan was ineligible to run because he had not filed “in good faith for the purpose of genuinely pursuing election” and had instead sought to “confuse or mislead” voters. The letter noted, for example, his request to be identified as “Dan,” like the incumbent. It also alluded to the subject matter of the Times story, highlighting his work with a political consultant who is a “known longtime supporter of Democratic candidates,” including Peltola. Sullivan appealed, and in June, an Alaska trial court judge reversed the Division of Elections’ decision. The gist of Judge Thomas A. Matthews’ order, which the Alaska Supreme Court later affirmed, is that under state law, the qualifications to run for Senate straightforwardly track the language of the U.S. Constitution: Candidates must be at least 30 years old, have been citizens for at least nine years, and live in the state they seek to represent. Given that Daniel J. Sullivan checks all three boxes, Matthews found that officials had no authority to invent an additional “good faith” requirement and use it to “exclude an otherwise qualified candidate.” Matthews’ opinion does not suggest that he is naive about what’s going on here. But critical to his analysis is the fact that under state law, the Division of Elections could have previously taken steps to prevent this from happening but didn’t: He points out that other states have dealt with same-name races, and that many have rules for weighing the “good faith” of prospective candidates. Since Alaska officials “could have predicted this situation would eventually arise,” Matthews concluded, they “may not now assert a new requirement, standard, limitation, or qualification after presentation of the candidate’s declaration.” The attention generated by the Times story has Republicans looking to impose consequences that go well beyond the confines of Alaska election law. The challenge they face is that, as the Times acknowledged, ballot-stacking techniques are “common in politics,” are “generally considered legal,” and have been “employed by both parties in Alaska.” Peltola knows this better than most: In 2024, when she lost her bid for reelection to the House, Republicans went to court to ensure that Eric Hafner, who is serving a 20-year federal prison sentence in New York for threatening to kill judges, remained on the ballot as a Democrat. During the primary campaign in this very election, outside groups were pouring money into the state, hoping to sabotage Peltola by placing other Democrats on the general election ballot. Intuitively, recruiting a candidate with the same name as another candidate feels like a more nefarious form of ballot stacking. But so far, Republicans’ efforts to draw a legal distinction have been vague, speculative, nonsensical, or some combination thereof. On Truth Social, for example, Trump claimed that the Times had revealed that Democrats had lied “under oath in Federal Court,” which is an accurate description of a crime, but not a thing that happened here. The Wall Street Journal reported that federal prosecutors are “exploring” whether to “bring charges alleging a conspiracy to violate voters’ rights.” There are real laws that protect these rights, but prosecutors generally use them to target people who threaten voters or try to trick them into not voting—for example, this guy, who in 2016 posted memes urging Hillary Clinton supporters to “vote” via text message. (Even that conviction was overturned on appeal.) Whatever you think of Daniel J. Sullivan’s candidacy, it is not an illegal effort to stop people from voting: He is running for office, under his real name, in the state where he lives. Anyone who gets “tricked” into voting for him (and only for him) is still, in fact, voting. Even if one of the more ambitious Trump prosecutors decides to give it a shot, the First Amendment will present serious challenges, since going after candidates and their supporters for ostensibly harboring ulterior motives would criminalize a lot of things that parties in this country already do. In context, the threats Republicans are issuing right now are best understood not as fully baked legal theories but as performances intended to convey to Trump that they, too, are angry about getting played and are looking into it very strongly. Dunleavy, Alaska’s governor, told the Wall Street Journal that he informed Trump that he is “investigating at the state level,” which is what I would tell the incensed leader of my party if I knew he wanted me to do something but there was little I could do and less than a week in which to do it. The more likely (and more serious) implication of the story is the possibility of Trump and his allies using it as the basis of a new round of “stolen election” allegations. By loudly investigating the matter now, they are leaving open the possibility of challenging the results, not with oafish phone calls or a horde of Trump supporters storming the Capitol, but with the institutional power of the Justice Department. Citing unproven allegations to pressure Alaska officials not to certify a hypothetical Peltola victory—Dunleavy has said he is open to holding up the process, “depending upon the outcomes of this investigation”—feels like a long shot, but if control of the Senate comes down to Alaska, Trump is at least going to take it. The one bit of good news here is that if this Alaska Public Media report is any indication, actual voters do not care all that much. One described the allegations as “nothing that we haven’t heard before.” Others expressed frustration with all the media focus on the ballot, rather than the policy positions of the people on it. Still others understandably took offense at the notion that Alaskans are not smart enough to figure out how to vote. This is shady business. But for better and for worse, in this country, “shady” is just how politics works. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. 'Mother of all editors' Emacs is 50
2026-11-01

'Mother of all editors' Emacs is 50

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
By a startling coincidence, so is its arch-rival Vi No lesser authority than the MIT Technology Review says that the Emacs editor has turned 50 years old. But then, it is biased — Emacs was invented in MIT's AI lab. Simson Garfinkel's article is simply called EMACS turns 50, but the sub-heading explains why it matters more: "How the MIT AI Lab's text editor gave birth to the free software movement." There are multiple timelines and accounts of the origins of Emacs, and it is not a single monolithic application. The name is an abbreviation for "Editing MACroS", and the original editor for which this set of macros was developed was TECO – "Text Editor and COrrector," written by Dan Murphy in 1962. Murphy died earlier this year at the age of 83. The inimitable Jamie Zawinski has an encyclopedic Emacs Timeline – as he mentions at the start, his interest being that he long ago maintained a fork of GNU Emacs, originally called Lucid Emacs. It's still around, under its later name XEmacs. There are a lot of forks and re-implementations of Emacs out there, and we are not going to go into all of them, or this article would become a book – one only interesting to programmer-archeologists (to use a term coined by the late great Vernor Vinge). As for who originally wrote those Editing Macros, though, another history has quotations from several of the people involved: On the Origin of Emacs in 1976. (Note – some of the quotes still contain the | symbols that marked quoted text.) This credits Lisp boffin Guy L. Steele Jr with the original work in 1975, but in it, Steele says: "RMS is responsible for the names 'E' and 'EMACS'. RMS still deserves 99% or 99.9% or 99.99% or 99.999% of the credit for taking a package of TECO macros and turning it into the most powerful editor on the planet." RMS is of course Richard Stallman, and the page also quotes some emails from RMS on October 23 and 31, 1976. Mind you, just a few years after the event, in a 1981 talk now preserved on the GNU website, Stallman said, "I began the development of EMACS in 1974 with an improvement to TECO". Like all the best FOSS, even in its very early stages, it was a collaborative effort – but around late October 1976 is when RMS took over the bulk of the development. Of the many forks of Emacs over its half a century of development, some are still just about hanging on in there. When we looked up the link for XEmacs, we were surprised to see that there was a new release: XEmacs 21.5.37 "lettuce" appeared just last month. And even the forks have forks: in this case, SXEmacs, last updated in 2020. Most of the changes in XEmacs and SXEmacs eventually made their way back upstream to GNU Emacs, which is still in active development, although as The Register reported in 2008, RMS is no longer the primary maintainer. GNU Emacs 31.1 appeared in August. Cards on the metaphorical table: this vulture is not an Emacs user. We have tried. A major influence was one of the Reg FOSS desk's favorite writers, Neal Stephenson, who in 1999 published an essay called In the Beginning… Was the Command Line. (It was later republished as a rather short book, but the text is still online.) In it, he says: "In the GNU/Linux world there are two major text editing programs: the minimalist vi (known in some implementations as elvis) and the maximalist emacs. I use emacs, which might be thought of as a thermonuclear word processor. It was created by Richard Stallman; enough said. It is written in Lisp, which is the only computer language that is beautiful. It is colossal, and yet it only edits straight ASCII text files, which is to say, no fonts, no boldface, no underlining. In other words, the engineer-hours that, in the case of Microsoft Word, were devoted to features like mail merge, and the ability to embed feature-length motion pictures in corporate memoranda, were, in the case of emacs, focused with maniacal intensity on the deceptively simple-seeming problem of editing text. If you are a professional writer–i.e., if someone else is getting paid to worry about how your words are formatted and printed–emacs outshines all other editing software in approximately the same way that the noonday sun does the stars." Emacs is 50-plus-year-old software, and it shows. This is a huge and complicated program: decades ago, the joke was that EMACS stood for "Eight Megs And Constantly Swapping". It is not an easy program to learn. Despite lavish documentation and a built-in tutorial, this is software so old that its terminology predates microprocessors and the personal computer. Its exhaustive manual is 804 pages long, and to pick some trivial examples, its terms for parts of the screen use “frame” to mean what’s now called a window, and “window” to mean what’s now called a pane. In the section on Keys, it discusses the Control and Meta keys. That’s a slight snag: you don’t have a Meta key, and neither do the authors of Emacs. It was a key on the now almost legendarily rare Space Cadet Keyboard, of which the Deskthority keyboard wiki says: “It is known as the holy grail of keyboards.” You can buy a modern replica, the Keymacs keyboard. They are hand-made in the Czech Republic, and depending on exactly what model and specifications you want, they cost from €2,998 for the Lispsaver up to €6,878 for the A624N Space Cadet. In other words, you can have a keyboard with a real Meta key if you want, but it is going to cost you. One of the problems with these historical terms, unfamiliar to anyone who wasn’t already programming in the 1970s, is that meanings drift. In the context of Emacs, "Meta" means Alt, or Opt on Macs. However, KDE also uses the Meta key – but what the KDE documentation calls "Meta" is the Windows key – or Command on Macs. Like most Linux folks, the GNOME documentation calls it the Super key. Names are important. They matter. Who's right, the Emacs folks or the KDE folks? Well, neither: nobody has Meta keys any more. We have Ctrl, Alt, and Cmd/Super/Windows. There have been valiant efforts to modernize Emacs and bring it into line with modern UI and terminology. This vulture tried to learn Aquamacs, which modernized Emacs with a native macOS UI, but sadly it's no longer maintained. Another, which we enjoyed trying, is ErgoEmacs, which changes the key bindings and rearranges the UI into something much more like the IBM Common User Access arrangement, as used in Windows and most Linux desktops. However, it never really caught on and you need to know your way around Emacs to install it and configure it for your regional keyboard layout. So complex is it to configure Emacs that there are multiple pre-configured distributions of Emacs, such as Doom Emacs, or the minimalist Emacs Solo. We wish someone did a "CUA-Macs" config, with ErgoEmacs  integrated and able to pick up your existing keyboard config. Come to that, the Gershwin Desktop project that we mentioned along with GhostBSD last year is in active development, and it's based around GNUstep. Maybe its developers could pick up Aquamacs, and adapt it to GNUstep. There is a long history of such Emacs facelifts. One instance is Borland’s Sprint word-processor, one of the selling points of which was that it could emulate the user interfaces of most of the leading MS-DOS word-processors of its day (just before they all converged on IBM CUA). Before Borland acquired it, Sprint was called The Final Word, developed by musical equipment makers Mark of the Unicorn. FinalWord was based on an Emacs clone called MINCE. Another well-known Emacs distribution is Spacemacs, which adapts Emacs to have a more Vi-like user interface. Vi is the other seminal Unix text editor, as the Stephenson quote above mentions. As this article on Origins and the Evolution of Vi & Vim notes, it too dates back to 1976. Indeed, that’s the reason we chose the year in this story: Tilde, a text editor that doesn’t work like it’s 1976. One feature of Tilde we enjoy is that it supports use of the mouse even on the Linux console – which is the headline new feature of GNU Emacs 31.1. Vi is very much alive and well today. GNU Emacs is based on the original source code, but Vi mainly survives as re-implementations. The recreation Stephenson mentions, Elvis, lives on today in the form of nvi. However, by far the best known is Vim, maintained by Bram Moolenaar until his death in 2023. In the grand FOSS forking tradition, it too has forks, including Neovim, as used in Omarchy, and more recently, the AI-free Vim Classic – a new project led by Drew DeVault, who just launched the similarly LLM-free AlpineBSD. ® Legacy public switched telephone network service is to be retired on January 31, 2027 Experimental orbital datacenter prototype puts its TPU to work producing poetry PARTNER CONTENT: Michael Stonebraker and Bruce Momjian on why enterprise AI needs a database that no one controls, and what PostgreSQL 19 says about the road ahead Labeled in black on black - controls for a galactic hyper-hearse or ill-advised Windows theme? Landline phones will switch off across Britland in 100 days, no extensions – Openreach
2026-11-01

Landline phones will switch off across Britland in 100 days, no extensions – Openreach

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Legacy public switched telephone network service is to be retired on January 31, 2027 Openreach has issued a stark reminder to customers that the legacy phone network is being officially switched off on January 31, and says the deadline will not be postponed. The infrastructure arm of BT Group, Britain’s biggest telecoms biz, circulated the warning this week in a letter to comms providers that use its network to deliver their services. It informs them that any customers still on legacy public switched telephone network (PSTN) connections are at risk of disruption when these are retired at the end of January, 2027. The switch-off could affect businesses and consumers alike. Beyond the plain old telephone, systems that rely on PSTN connectivity include point-of-sale terminals, fire and security alarms, and personal care alarms for elderly and vulnerable people. The move is part of a wider government scheme to get the UK onto all-digital high-speed networks running on fiber-optic infrastructure, and the legacy phone service - with its dependency on copper cabling - was standing in the way. Many households have now ditched landline handsets and instead use only mobile phones, yet for those who still want a landline, the changeover means plugging the phone into the back of a broadband router to access a Digital Voice service. Openreach has been planning this move for the best part of a decade. It has already pushed back the deadline at least once, after the government asked telecoms providers not to force vulnerable customers who use phone-network systems such as TeleCare to switch until fixes are in place. Nevertheless, public awareness seems low that analog phone services will soon be disconnected. "Openreach and all providers need to coordinate a much more cohesive campaign that reaches customers directly," said telecoms analyst Paolo Pescatore at PP Foresight. "TV, billboards and newspaper advertising should reinforce a consistent message, supported by targeted letters, calls and door-to-door support for older and vulnerable people who need extra help," he told The Register. "Customers need to understand what is changing, what action to take, and who will help them. Providers must take responsibility for ensuring people remain safely connected, especially those who rely on their landline or a care alarm." Caroline Abrahams, charity director at Age UK, agreed. "With only three months to go, the digital landline switchover is entering a critical final phase. Most people have moved over without major problems, but nobody who relies on their landline or TeleCare should be left behind," she told us. "Anyone with concerns should contact their provider, but the responsibility cannot rest with customers alone. Providers must not give up on people who have not responded. They need to keep trying to reach them and offer practical, individual support, recognizing that some may be unwell, living with dementia, worried about scams or without anyone nearby to help." Openreach has a webpage with information regarding the switch-off. This advises consumers to call their service provider as soon as possible to ensure service is not interrupted. The company previously told The Register that customers without a fiber connection will likely be switched to SOTAP for Analog (Single Order Transitional Access Product), where equipment is installed in the local telephone exchange to allow them to continue to use their old phone line as before. Openreach has warned that commercial customers delaying migration risk significant disruption and unnecessary costs - leaving it until the last minute could make it harder to secure the requisite engineering support before the deadline hits. "Businesses and organizations that delay action are making things harder for themselves," said James Lilley, Openreach Director of All-IP. "The straightforward migrations have largely been completed. What's left are the complex connections that underpin critical operations, requiring careful planning, supplier coordination, and thorough testing. We are urging businesses to act now, audit their remaining analog services, and work with their communications provider on a migration plan today." Earlier this year, the firm tried to drive home the message by announcing it was stepping up its charges to service providers and companies still operating legacy business lines, who will by now be paying double the rental cost compared to last year. ® Legacy public switched telephone network service is to be retired on January 31, 2027 Experimental orbital datacenter prototype puts its TPU to work producing poetry PARTNER CONTENT: Michael Stonebraker and Bruce Momjian on why enterprise AI needs a database that no one controls, and what PostgreSQL 19 says about the road ahead Labeled in black on black - controls for a galactic hyper-hearse or ill-advised Windows theme? Google's AI satellite sends a very expensive haiku to Earth
2026-10-31

Google's AI satellite sends a very expensive haiku to Earth

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Experimental orbital datacenter prototype puts its TPU to work producing poetry Google on Friday celebrated receiving a poem from its first experimental orbital AI datacenter satellite. The company's Suncatcher satellite, launched at the start of the month and put into low Earth orbit, has responded to a ground station prompt to "Write a haiku from the perspective of a TPU orbiting Earth, with the wonderful world spinning beneath you." Travis Beals, senior director of Google's interdisciplinary Paradigms of Intelligence research team, said that with Suncatcher's host and TPU trays powered up and machine learning tests completed, the onboard Gemma 4 model achieved its "Hello World" milestone by sending an inference response from orbit. "During a ground-station pass, we uplinked our first prompt to our onboard Gemma 4 open model, asking it to generate a haiku about being in space," wrote Beals in a blog post. And as the satellite passed over Svalbard, Norway, the orbiting AI model responded over the downlink: Those roughly 20 tokens would cost about $0.000012 if generated through the Google Cloud API, based on Gemma 4 26B's pricing of $0.15 per million input tokens and $0.60 per million output tokens, excluding input costs. Add in a satellite weighing perhaps 500 kg, based on the mass of SpaceX's Starlink satellites, and a retail launch price of around $7,000 per kg, and you have a very expensive haiku. According to Google's calculations, if the cost of getting a satellite into low Earth orbit can be brought down to $200/kg, the launch cost amortized over the satellite's useful life (~$810–7,500/kW/year) could be comparable to current electricity spending for terrestrial datacenters in the US (~$570–3,000/kW/year). Google predicts that SpaceX could achieve that by 2035, more or less, and considers it strategically beneficial to prototype well in advance of that actually happening. After the haiku, Beals said, "we tested space-themed limericks and telemetry prompts reporting the technical status of the payload systems." Next up: tests for evaluating how TPUs perform under thermal stress and radiation. Google has already done some laboratory testing of its Trillium Cloud TPU and AMD host server to see how well they can withstand a 67 MeV proton beam. The system's high-bandwidth memory shows the most sensitivity in terms of the total ionizing dose – it exhibits irregularities after a cumulative dose of 2 krad(Si), which is about 3× Google's minimum requirement for TID survival of 750 rad(Si). "For typical transformer workloads, the observed SDC [silent data corruption] rate of approximately one event/17 rad, at an estimated orbital dose of 150 rad(Si)/year, translates to a very low failure probability on the order of 1 per 3 million inferences, assuming 1 inference per second," Google researchers said in a paper published earlier this month. "While this error rate is likely acceptable for inference, the impact of SEEs on training jobs, and the efficacy of system-level mitigations, requires further study." Indeed, the study will continue. Next year, the plan is to test a pair of interconnected satellites. Rather than lofting large satellites into orbit, Google anticipates sending a constellation of many smaller satellites that communicate with one another. ® Legacy public switched telephone network service is to be retired on January 31, 2027 Experimental orbital datacenter prototype puts its TPU to work producing poetry PARTNER CONTENT: Michael Stonebraker and Bruce Momjian on why enterprise AI needs a database that no one controls, and what PostgreSQL 19 says about the road ahead Labeled in black on black - controls for a galactic hyper-hearse or ill-advised Windows theme? Goth aesthetic gone wild: a user's self-inflicted black screen of non-death in Windows 3.1’s
2026-10-31

Goth aesthetic gone wild: a user's self-inflicted black screen of non-death in Windows 3.1’s

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Labeled in black on black - controls for a galactic hyper-hearse or ill-advised Windows theme? Microsoft senior software engineer Raymond Chen has explained what Windows is doing when you hold down the power button, and shared a story about the time a customer accidentally created a Goth Mode for Windows 3.1. There's a section in The Hitchhiker's Guide to the Galaxy (actually, page 116 of this writer's dog-eared copy of The Restaurant at the End of the Universe) in which Zaphod Beeblebrox observes of a ship's interior: "Every time you operate one of these weird black controls that are labeled in black on black background, a little black light lights up black to let you know you've done it." The same turned out to be true of a user who called Microsoft customer support to complain that after Windows 3.1 booted up, all that remained after the product logo disappeared was a blank screen. It turned out the customer liked black a little too much and, according to Chen, made themself a Goth theme. The customer support engineer on Microsoft's end pieced it together. The customer had, according to Chen, "set the wallpaper color to black. They set the title bar color to black. They set the button color to black. They set the window background color to black. They set the window text color to black. "Every possible color you could pick in the Colors control panel was set to black." We suspect the mouse pointer, at least, would have stuck around, unless the customer had also managed to change some settings around this. And then there are the icons… However, Windows 3.1 was a long time ago, and this writer's attempts at theming then were the consequences of growing up in the '70s and '80s, which resulted in some interesting neon, orange, and brown combinations. As for Goth mode, Chen said, "Once you get yourself into this situation, it’s hard to get out because you can't see anything." One solution he suggested was deleting the [colors] section from the win.ini file. Chen told the anecdote as a coda to the question – what exactly is modern Windows doing when you hold down the power button on a PC? According to Chen, after eight seconds of holding the power button, the Windows kernel realizes a forced power-off is happening and tries to take a kernel dump for diagnostics. He explained that the kernel dumps were classified by Microsoft as "long power button hold" (LPBH) failures, with one of the bits of information captured indicating if all the pixels on the screen were black. The video driver might therefore be the culprit. Or whatever was responsible for drawing to the screen. Today's Windows struggles with dark mode. But in the dim, distant past, every pixel being black might mean the user was either Hotblack Desiato or had done something very silly indeed. ® Legacy public switched telephone network service is to be retired on January 31, 2027 Experimental orbital datacenter prototype puts its TPU to work producing poetry PARTNER CONTENT: Michael Stonebraker and Bruce Momjian on why enterprise AI needs a database that no one controls, and what PostgreSQL 19 says about the road ahead Labeled in black on black - controls for a galactic hyper-hearse or ill-advised Windows theme? Amazon sends chills through its workforce with mixed message memo about AI use
2026-10-31

Amazon sends chills through its workforce with mixed message memo about AI use

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
A lot of software engineers feel bleak about the future of the profession There's a ridiculously simple test you can use to figure out whether AI mandates at a given company are well-considered, or are being shoved out the door by out-of-touch buffoons: What happens when an engineer documents that AI made a task worse? Are they taken seriously, or are they marginalized and eventually managed out? Conveniently, this week Amazon published a treatise by Terk Terkowitz, a VP/Distinguished Engineer/Final Boss of Their Independent Contributor Technical Career Track, on the role of the developer in the age of AI. It was originally an internal missive sent to their software engineers and their managers back in September, which is an interesting lag for a document that sounds this confident. It also maps exceedingly well to what other organizations are struggling with. I think it's a good document, but I can't help but view it with a somewhat cynical eye. I see it as an attempt to make the future of the profession of software engineering feel less bleak. And it's feeling mighty bleak for a lot of folks right now. This treatise comes at a time of dramatic upheaval. Tech as an industry is no stranger to change (usually at 3AM, heralded by the pager), but something about the current AI-ification of the world strikes many of us as fundamentally dissimilar from previous revolutions. Folks are scared about their career prospects. "Should I give up tech and get into farming?" is a sincere question multiple people have asked me. I don't know! Tech is already a goat rodeo, so you've got a leg up if you do?This anxiety isn't theoretical. In May, Amazon wisely shut down KiroRank, an employee-built cash incineration leaderboard that ranked people by token usage, after the obvious thing happened: people spun up junk tasks to climb the ranks. This is now called "tokenmaxxing," and the Terkowitz treatise specifically calls it out as being at odds with "frugality." Meta did the same thing with "Claudenomics" and 85K employees that same week. And Amazon reportedly announced another 250 job cuts this week. I can't tell you that these things are related. But I can tell you that every engineer who read the memo assumes that they are. This document contains a lot of great stuff. I have a tremendous level of respect, both personal and professional, for Amazon's Principal Engineering community; I'm led to understand that that respect may not be reciprocated, but that's okay. I am, by their standards, a lapsed engineer who ran away to join the circus as one of its lesser clowns. Terkowitz's memo emphasizes customer outcomes and engineering judgement, two things I would absolutely not delegate to AI any time soon. (Note: I am writing this in October of 2026, with the full understanding that previous comments on the capabilities where AI falls short tend to age like fine milk.) It documents work that (today) AI tools aren't improving. That's way better than assuming that every objection to AI inherently comes from some self-interested Luddite who's scared of being replaced. It also says something smart about measurement: look at the 90th percentile of AI adoption, at those teams burning the most tokens, and check whether their delivery actually went up commensurately. This is great! Shout it from the rooftops; we should all be doing such things. The memo says “If your work does not benefit from current AI tooling, that is a finding, not a failing.” Document it, bring it to your manager, work through the problem. I agree with every word of that! The question is whether anyone's promo doc is gonna survive citing it. Here's what happens next. The memo concedes that input metrics are "deeply flawed," but then in the next paragraph says to go ahead and use them anyway, carefully. It then warns that non-adopters are falling behind. Those are three different positions within three sentences and they're not at all aligned with one another. That's not muddled; I think it's cover. A manager can cite "a finding, not a failing" when a strong engineer skips the AI tool, and then cite "falling behind" when it's time to PIP someone. Both are congruent with their stated policy; you get to decide which interpretation you want to go with in a given situation. How will managers handle justified AI non-use? Are promotion and performance decisions being made in a way that rewards these long term outcomes? Or are they instead taking the easy weasel way out, optimizing for what's cheap to measure, and rewarding tool activity? Because if you give me a metric, I will turn it into a target. So will an overwhelming majority of employees. When that happens, everyone loses — it's how KiroRank happened in the first place. I write this after two days of attending GitHub Universe, where they talked about how agents start roughly 40% of PRs now. And yet, Jay Parikh’s keynote argument was that useful output wasn't "more code," but rather completed outcomes: features shipped, bugs fixed, security issues addressed, loudmouth columnists silenced, etc. The problem here is that their entire business model cuts against that; they're billing in a model that charges customers on a consumption basis, not an output basis. There's nothing, today, that ties "AI spend" to "output that was worth the effort." Which brings us back to Amazon. The memo says tokenmaxxing is at odds with frugality, and for Amazon’s internal cost line, it is. Meanwhile AWS bills Bedrock and Kiro customers by the token. Inside the building, tokenmaxxing is a problem to be stamped out. Outside it, it’s the revenue plan. ® Legacy public switched telephone network service is to be retired on January 31, 2027 Experimental orbital datacenter prototype puts its TPU to work producing poetry PARTNER CONTENT: Michael Stonebraker and Bruce Momjian on why enterprise AI needs a database that no one controls, and what PostgreSQL 19 says about the road ahead Labeled in black on black - controls for a galactic hyper-hearse or ill-advised Windows theme? London's Oxford Street signage shows its underwear to passing tourists Today's bit of signage silage is either Windows 10 protesting that it is not part of London's Oxford Street pedestrianization, or someone failing to heed an alert before the public is made all too aware that things aren't going well… Eagle-eyed Register reader Aaron Colwill spotted the sign outside the food hall entrance of a Marks & Spencer store on London's Oxford Street. New Zelda and GTA represent a games industry that no longer exists
2026-11-01

New Zelda and GTA represent a games industry that no longer exists

The Verge | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
The two biggest games of the year launch in November, offering some reprieve from a terrible year.﻿ The two biggest games of the year launch in November, offering some reprieve from a terrible year.﻿ This is The Stepback, a weekly newsletter breaking down one essential story from the tech world. For more on the biggest game releases of the year, follow Andrew Webster. The Stepback arrives in our subscribers’ inboxes on Sunday at 8AM ET. Opt in for The Stepbackhere. The two biggest games of 2026 are set to launch just a few weeks apart in November. On the one side you have the behemoth that is Grand Theft Auto VI, an experience 13 years in the making, and the sequel to one of the best-selling games in history. Its predecessor, GTA V, has sold a staggering 230 million copies since it launched in 2013, and was re-released across three generations of consoles. Developer Rockstar is a pioneer in the realm of open-world games, dating back to GTA III, and the scale and ambitions of those worlds have steadily grown since then. With GTA V and later Red Dead Redemption 2, the studio’s ideas reached a new level of realism. And the sixth GTA looks poised to eclipse them both, which has created an unprecedented level of demand. Analyst firm Circana estimates that 29 percent of gamers in the US intend to buy the game, with demand so large it could boost flagging console sales. “Many consumers who have yet to upgrade to a current-generation console may choose to purchase a PlayStation 5 or Xbox Series specifically to play the title,” analyst Mat Piscatella says in a statement. GTA VI’s reach is so vast that almost every other major game launched early to avoid it. The one exception is Nintendo. The company’s tentpole release for the holidays, and its next major exclusive for the Switch 2, is a lavish remake of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time. The release is notable for a few reasons. For one thing, it’s an attempt from Nintendo to modernize one of the most influential and beloved games in its long history. The original Ocarina of Time was the world’s first introduction to a 3D iteration of Hyrule, which, in turn, made it an early glimpse at what was possible in three-dimensional spaces, laying a blueprint for countless games that came afterward. Nintendo has since expanded on that, turning Switch-era adventures Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom into wide-open adventures designed for player creativity. They went on to become some of the best-selling games for Nintendo’s most successful console. Ocarina of Time’s launch also comes just ahead of a live-action Zelda movie in 2027, which could catapult the franchise to a new level of prominence. That’s why Nintendo is seemingly the only company unafraid of competing with GTA. These aren’t just two blockbuster games – they’re two series with the pull to make this a real cultural moment, akin to “Barbenheimer” but for video games. They’re the kind of launches that make it onto Saturday Night Live and have NBA players worried. It’s just too bad about the rest of the industry. These major November releases are something of a mirage, two outliers that stand out amid the wreckage that is the rest of the video game industry. While there have been plenty of major releases and great games this year, the main theme of 2026 has been just how dire things have become. Layoffs, studio closures, unpopular business models, the ever-looming threat of AI, and massive increases in hardware costs have combined to create an ominous cloud over the medium. Here is just a snapshot of what the year has been like: Xbox is in the middle of a “reset” that involves laying off thousands of developers, shutting down some studios, and gutting others; Fortnite developer Epic cut more than 1,000 jobs due to a sharp downturn in the live-service games space, and it’s currently going all-in on vibe coding; Sony is also struggling, slashing jobs at Destiny developer Bungie, watching console sales plummet as costs skyrocket, and raising the ire of gamers by moving on from physical media; and meanwhile, major publishers like EA and Ubisoft are in their own dramatic states of upheaval. And the bad news hasn’t really let up. Just last week, EA shut down beloved studio BioWare and canceled the next Mass Effect game, and this week Sony studio Sucker Punch cut staff despite the success of last year’s Ghost of Yōtei. Every day, it seems, brings some new unwelcome development. Clearly, things are changing for the video game industry; they have to change. What that future looks like, though, remains unclear. The next generation of game consoles is being threatened by exorbitant prices, and all the while the rise of AI-powered tools is sending ripples across the industry, threatening the established order between players and creators as games are ported to every platform imaginable. “These are unmistakably good outcomes for players, even when (and sometimes because) they challenge our existing business models,” Xbox CEO Asha Sharma said in a recent memo. But how that plays out remains to be seen. It’s not all doom and gloom. There have been rare success stories, where studios like FromSoftware and Remedy have managed to carve out a sustainable existence in a turbulent field. But for the most part, the established norms that have been part of the blockbuster games industry for years are going away. Big games are too expensive to make, hardware is too expensive to buy (and the cost is only going up), and somewhere, something has to give. It’s tempting to look at Zelda and GTA as answers to these problems. After all, if Nintendo and Rockstar are doing so well, there must be a solution out there. But they’re also two very specific cases that are essentially impossible to replicate. Nintendo maintains ownership over all aspects of its platform, from the hardware to the games, and it has taken a careful and largely risk-averse approach that has allowed it to weather the current storm and create a growing entertainment empire. Rockstar, meanwhile, is the rare example of being too big to fail. GTA V was so massive for so long — Circana says it was among the top 20 best-selling games in the US for 13 straight years — that it allowed the studio to build up both an unprecedented amount of anticipation and, it seems, a game big enough to match it. In that way, Zelda and GTA are throwbacks to a time when major games were sure bets. Their duelling releases are fascinating both because these games are so impactful, but also because, with all of the impending upheaval, the industry might never see a moment like this again.
