Prop 45 in California could be hiding a loophole for data centers
2026-11-01

Prop 45 in California could be hiding a loophole for data centers

The Verge | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Proposition 45 makes it easier for developers to speed through environmental reviews. Proposition 45 makes it easier for developers to speed through environmental reviews. A high-stakes California ballot measure pitched as a fast track for “essential” infrastructure could wind up giving data center developers a loophole to evade public scrutiny, environmental advocates warn. Proposition 45 would shorten timelines for state-mandated environmental assessments for eligible projects and make it more difficult for residents to challenge those projects in court. Proponents say passing the measure would help alleviate the state’s housing shortage and meet rising electricity demand with new renewable energy projects. Environmental groups and some legal experts, however, warn that data center developers backing the proposition could escape scrutiny if Prop 45 becomes law. “The concern here is that data center developers will find a way to shoehorn their projects into the definition of an essential project and then get to take advantage of … the fast-track review that it’ll create,” says Fernando Gaytan, a senior attorney at the nonprofit environmental law organization Earthjustice, which opposes Prop 45. “Communities get cut out of the conversation.” “Communities get cut out of the conversation.” Prop 45 would loosen standards for certain projects subject to the California Environmental Quality Act (CEQA). CEQA requires state agencies to perform environmental reviews and work with project developers to find ways to limit any harmful impacts to the environment and public health. Crucially, CEQA also mandates that state agencies gather community input and consider public comment before issuing any approvals. Specifically, Prop 45 would create a 365-day deadline for agencies to complete the environmental impact report for a project. It also stipulates that any legal challenges would need to be settled within 270 days. It would only allow for 45 days of public comment, and it would only require state agencies and developers to consult with Native American tribes that have been federally recognized. CEQA as it’s currently written sets a minimum rather than a maximum number of days for public review, and mandates consultation with tribes that might be impacted by a project even if they’re not formally recognized by the federal government as having “government-to-government” relations with the US. During the CEQA review process, state agencies develop alternatives that developers can take to mitigate environmental and health risks. Prop 45 would turn that on its head, making developers responsible for coming up with a single alternative project plan for regulators to consider. The proposition would also strip away an agency’s authority to repeatedly ask a developer for more information about a proposed project; state regulators would need to specify all the information a company is required to provide at the start of the application process. On top of all that, Prop 45 would bar courts from halting a project altogether even if it finds that the regulatory agency failed to correctly comply with CEQA. The judge would only have the ability to pause specific parts of a project that are problematic, rather than forcing a full stop to construction. That’s the piece that’s most egregious for Gaytan. “It’s hard to undo harms once you’ve already started a project,” he says. Data centers are a hot-button election issue for midterms across the US and have become a point of contention for Prop 45 as well. The proposition lists nine types of “essential” projects that would be eligible for streamlined review, including housing, clean energy, and “broadband Internet access.” Gaytan and some other legal experts contend that the latter designation — broadband internet access — could include data centers. The language includes any project that would “provide mass-market retail service by wire service, wireless service, or radio to customers in this state that provides the capability to transmit data to, and receive data from, all or substantially all internet endpoints, including, but not limited to, any capabilities that are incidental to and enable the operation of the communications service.” Proposed data centers that include any broadband transmission infrastructure might be able to squeeze into that definition, UC Davis School of Law professor emeritus Richard Frank and attorney Matthew McKerley write in a recent column in the Daily Journal. They also point out that companies currently developing data centers have poured hundreds of thousands of dollars into a campaign to support Prop 45. And while Prop 45 excludes certain types of projects, like nuclear energy and dial-up internet access service, it doesn’t explicitly bar data centers. Proponents, meanwhile, argue that Prop 45 would not apply to data centers. “The fact that data centers may play a role in supporting the Internet does not make them ‘essential broadband Internet access projects’ under Prop 45,” attorney and lobbyist Kurt Oneto writes in The National Law Review. The Yes on Prop 45 campaign didn’t immediately respond to a request for comment. Prop 45 has managed to garner support from the California and Hawaii chapter of the NAACP, which has been among one of the most vocal civil rights organizations to oppose data center developments across the US. The NAACP filed suit against xAI over air pollution from gas turbines at its Colossus 2 data center, and the Texas chapter has called for a statewide moratorium on data center development. “Working families and communities of color pay the highest price when essential projects like housing, clean energy, water, health care facilities, and transportation take years longer and cost millions more than they should,” Rick Callender, president of NAACP California/Hawaii State Conference, said in a September 9th press release. “Prop. 45 is about accountability, not shortcuts, creating a smarter process that gets these projects built sooner while protecting civil rights and environmental safeguards.” Regardless of whether Prop 45 influences data center development in California, its opponents worry that it could disempower the same communities that the new projects it covers are supposed to benefit. “It’s about public participation and democracy,” Gaytan says. “It’s about neighbors being able to say, ‘I want you to be transparent with me about what’s going behind that wall.’” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Get in the spooky spirit with these horrors from YouTube
2026-10-31

Get in the spooky spirit with these horrors from YouTube

The Verge | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Close out the Halloween season with these creepy, weird, and unsettling videos from YouTube. Close out the Halloween season with these creepy, weird, and unsettling videos from YouTube. Rather than recommend a single horror film or an unsettling album for our final installment of spooky season recs, I present you with a small selection of creepy, disturbing, and just plain weird videos from YouTube. There’s a little something for everyone here. Whether you’re a fan of surreal art house shorts, cosmic horror, or classic viral clips, you’re bound to find something below that will get you in the Halloween spirit. This 1983 short film from Cecelia Condit found its audience over 30 years later when it was uploaded to YouTube and eventually found its way to Reddit. If you’ve ever wondered what a feminist musical about cannibals directed by David Lynch would be like, wonder no more. Possibly in Michigan is only 12 minutes long, but it has inspired hours of YouTube videos dedicated to analyzing its deeply unsettling imagery and meaning. The line between horror and comedy is razor thin, and Homemade Gatorade expertly threads that line. It’s bizarre, uncomfortable, and uncanny. It blends stiff animation with heavily processed video to create something quite unique. I’m never sure if I should be laughing or squirming in terror with every turn this road trip short takes. Big budget cosmic horror films often make the same mistake — they try to show too much. What makes Portrait of God work is it knows less is more. So much of its short runtime is spent staring into an empty abyss. Or is it empty… Speaking of staring intently at nothing and wondering if you did actually see anything, Heck is the short that eventually was turned into the experimental horror Skinamarink. Personally, I love Skinamarink, but I understand it’s not for everyone. 100 minutes is a long time to be staring into staticky corners of a drab suburban home. Heck delivers the same tension-building quiet, but in a much more digestible 29 minutes. There’s not much to say about this one-minute loopable short from David B Earle. It’s the sort of video that did gangbusters on early YouTube. It’s all abstract creepiness and nightmare-fuel visuals. If you want the full effect and need something to put on the TV at your Halloween party, put on this four-hour looped version. No Halloween is complete without this banger from Nick Lutsko. Keyboards of The Verge
2026-10-31

Keyboards of The Verge

The Verge | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
From the strictly utilitarian to the ergonomic to the obsessive, these are the keyboards we use every day. If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. What is a keyboard if not the original brain-computer interface? You can tap on a touchscreen or talk to the computer, but if your work involves getting information out of your head and into the computer, you’re probably spending a lot of time using a keyboard. We’ve written about many keyboards over the years, from the legendary Optimus Maximus to the ultra-luxe Seneca to even stranger fare. But I wanted to know what my coworkers actually use. As I’d hoped, there’s a wide range. Some of us are strictly utilitarian, optimizing for ergonomics or functionality. Others obsess over every detail of feel, sound, layout, and looks. Still others — maybe the most enlightened — just use whatever’s in front of them. NuPhy came up a lot. So did Keychron. The Apple Magic Keyboard has its stalwarts. The Kinesis Freestyle split is surprisingly well represented. And there are other, odder entries. I currently use the Kinesis Freestyle Pro, and I’ve used it for so long that I’ve forgotten when I first got it. In the late 2010s, I had wrist pain that led me to try a lot of ergonomic keyboards so I could keep using computers. The Freestyle Pro — with its optional tenting add-ons that give the keyboard a significant angle and the palm rest accessories — just feels the best under my hands. If this keyboard broke, I’d buy another one the same day. For work, I use a simple Keychron K2. But for my gaming PC, I use the Logitech G713 wired mechanical keyboard — the one that comes with the cloud wrist rest. Did I mostly buy it for the wrist rest? Perhaps. But I do love the tenkeyless layout. My previous gaming keyboard had a number pad and two rows of programmable keys, which was cool, but I mostly used the number pad to type the alt code for em dashes. The G713, on the other hand, meets my gaming requirements without taking up half my desk. I’m not playing anything competitive on my PC that requires adjustable actuation points, so the GX red linear switches do the trick. (And they’re the right amount of clicky, unlike the overly loud ones on my K2.) So yeah, maybe I bought it for the sleek all-white look and the cute wrist rest, but it’s also a great keyboard, too. I’m using Apple’s Magic Keyboard with the numeric keypad. Some version of Apple’s keyboard has been on my desk for over 20 years, starting with the Apple A1048 that came with my first Mac in college. I loved that keyboard (the sound of my fingertips hitting the keys — swoon!) and I wore it down to the point of keys falling off. Since then, I’ve stuck with Apple’s wireless keyboards. Honestly, it’s a pretty keyboard, but otherwise unremarkable, and blends in with my desk setup (which also is kind of the point, right?). My biggest complaints are that the key legends wear off faster than I’d like and the Bluetooth connection isn’t totally reliable. It’s not my favorite Apple product (by far), but after two decades of muscle memory, I’m not switching anytime soon. I really want to be a mechanical keyboard guy. I love typing on mechanical keyboards! I love the clackiness, the way they look on my desk, the huge personality upgrade that comes with them. But then I start typing notes during a call, and someone goes, “Sorry, what’s that noise in the background?” and I bail on my mechanical keyboard. I used Logitech’s MX Keys S for the last few years, and love that keyboard, but recently replaced it with an Apple Magic Keyboard (black keys, number pad) because I got tired of typing my 1Password password all the time. I bought a $200 keyboard for the Touch ID button. Ugh. My keyboard is probably the single dumbest thing I have ever spent money on, but it brings me joy every time I look at it. The setup matches a Boop Redux case with the GMK Dots keycaps and Silent Bluish White (V2) switches. I didn’t so much want to get deep into the mechanical keyboard hobby as I wanted to have this exact keyboard after I saw images of the Dots keycaps on the (original) Boop case floating around in around 2022. It looks cool, right? (Right?) From a practical standpoint, this is fully a downgrade from the Apple Magic Keyboard I was using before: It’s bigger and louder, with fewer keys, and I probably type a bit slower on it, too, owing to the longer key strokes. It’s also a few hundred dollars more expensive. But you know what? I think it looks really cool. Since I review laptops and occasionally mechanical keyboards, I’m constantly changing what I type on. But one of my personal keyboards that remains close to my heart is my TM680 (aka the Gamakay LK67). It’s a cheap bare-bones 65-percent kit I bought from AliExpress in 2021. I was inspired by the video that YouTuber Scott “Keybored” Kim did on it, so I dabbled in modding it. I started with basic Gateron Milky Yellow linear switches and some crummy knockoff Evangelion keycaps, but I later upgraded to a much nicer MT3 3277 keycap set. I then spent hours individually lubricating and adding switch films to each of the Gaterons, lubing the stabilizers, inserting some foam into the base of the chassis, and adding layers of painter’s tape to the bottom of the PCB. This combination made my basic build sound and feel much better, and it shaped my preference for deeper, “thockier”-sounding boards that I still have today. My old TM680 is easily outdone by just about any budget board these days. Many switches now arrive pre-lubed from the factory, and even a sub-$100 keyboard can sound and feel nicer right out of the box. But doing the mods myself felt special. I doubt I’ll ever bother with all that work again, because lubing and filming switches is just too tedious and time-consuming, but I’m glad I at least experienced it once. And no matter how many new keyboards I test, I intend on keeping my TM680. I used to buy pretty expensive gaming keyboards that I can’t even name now without digging deep through email archives for receipts. I know I once spent nearly $200 on a full-size Razer mechanical keyboard with a numpad that took up a ton of desk space. The key travel felt amazing and it had a great sound. But what I really remember was knocking over a full glass of water on it. Oops! Farewell, keyboard. Unfortunately, this happened not once, not twice, but three times for me with relatively expensive keyboards over the years. Yes, I should have learned to contain liquids in bottles on my desk. But now I’ve settled for cheap–but–still-good options the same way I’ve done with the several pairs of earbuds I’ve ruined over the years. (Former serial AirPods Pro user here, now fully on board the Nothing Ear train.) I’m currently rocking a Keychron K2 with a 75-percent layout. It took me a minute to get my 120-plus WPM back on a cramped keyboard, but now it works just fine and I like the look. I’ll probably splurge again someday on a really compelling set of keycaps. I use the laptop that is attached to the M2 MacBook Air I was issued by my employer. It’s right there whenever I need it, but I think they should make a version that’s covered in way fewer crumbs. I have a toxic relationship with my keyboard, a Corsair K55 RGB Pro that I picked up for half the price at a thrift store a few years ago. It’s a decent, budget-friendly keyboard that’s fine to use for gaming and writing. But recently, it’s been getting on my nerves. The keys no longer light up when I turn my computer on, and it sometimes doesn’t sync properly with my Corsair iCUE software. I can’t see what I’m pressing unless the keyboard is lit up (the keys are translucent), so I’ve had to resort to unplugging the keyboard and plugging it back in when I turn on my computer, which usually does the trick. The issue is probably software-related, but I might just get fed up enough to buy myself a fancy new mechanical keyboard. The latest version of a Verge favorite, this low-profile mechanical keyboard has long battery life, tri-mode wireless, hot-swappable low-profile switches, and remappability. I have never been a keyboard guy. I was content to use the one attached to my laptop or whatever wireless Apple version I could get for cheap. But I changed when a coworker turned me on to the NuPhy Air75 mechanical keyboard. The keys feel great, the “clack-clack” sound is so satisfying, and the overall design is retro without seeming outdated. That big yellow spacebar, the delightfully orange Enter button, the subtly aquamarine Escape key — chef’s kiss. Connectivity is robust and the battery lasts for days before needing to be recharged. It took some getting used to, but I chalk it up to not being able to use my left ring finger while typing since breaking it in a freak scooter accident years ago. But that’s a different story. A 65 percent gasket-mounted wired mechanical keyboard kit. Use the Design Lab to customize its top, bottom, switchplate, and accent colors, plate materials, and mechanical or electrocapacitive PCBs. (Switches, stabilizers, and keycaps sold separately) These days I mostly swap between a Leopold FC660C and a couple of Omnitype Bauer Lites. The Leopold is a Topre board I’ve had since 2017; I’ve modified it a fair bit over the years, with silencing rings, a new controller board, and Ursa keycaps. I fell in love with the unbeatable tactility of Topre switches early, but I love the customizability of regular mechanical keyboards too, so I’ve spent a lot of time over the past decade trying to find something that combines the best of both. I got most of the way there with my first Bauer Lite a couple years ago; its Kailh Deep Sea Silent Islet switches are linear, but they’re the closest to Topre I ever found in an MX-compatible switch. (They’re basically identical to the ones in the HHKB Studio). It’s also fluorescent orange, which just makes me happy. I built another one recently with DynaCap electro-capacitive switches and MTNU Space Cadet keycaps. Do I need three wired 65-percent keyboards with very similar layouts and pretty similar typing feel? Probably not. But I have them anyway. Also several other keyboards. I love trying out new mechanical keyboards (and have reviewed dozens of them), but for the last year straight I’ve been using a NuPhy Halo75 V2 as my daily driver. I’ve built several custom mechanical keyboards over the years, but I haven’t changed anything on this one yet. I may swap out the switches and keycaps at some point. NuPhy’s tactile Mint switches are fairly good, but I miss the Leobog Juggle V2 switches I have on a wired keyboard that used to be my daily driver. I’ve been pretty happy with how the Halo75 sounds and feels out of the box. I also appreciate that the design’s simple enough to not be distracting while still having a few pops of color. The battery life could be a bit better, but there’s not much to complain about overall — the build quality feels solid, the keycaps and switches have held up well over months of heavy typing, and the Halo75 works smoothly with both Mac and Linux. It also has Bluetooth and 2.4GHz wireless connectivity, which was a must-have for me since I like to hop between multiple devices from the same keyboard. I’ve had a great experience with the Halo75 so far, but I’d recommend it mainly for Mac users — macOS is where it really shines. It works fine for gaming, but I’d suggest configuring it with linear switches if you want to use it for gaming, rather than the tactile switches I went with. I started building mechanical keyboards a few years ago, and have a few in my quiver, but the one I use most often is a NovelKeys NK65 in white. This 65-percent keyboard is sturdy and compact, with just enough extra buttons for work or surfing the web, and arrow keys in case I want to sneak in some gaming. It’s a hot-swap board, so I change up the setup occasionally, but it’s currently equipped with SA Mizu keycaps and C3Equalz x TKC Dragonfruit tactile switches, with a matching desk pad and metal Escape key. I’ve upgraded my writing equipment since 2021, when I proudly displayed an Apple Magic Keyboard on top of a piece of cardboard on top of a MacBook with several nonfunctional butterfly keys. And as much as I love collecting dedicated typing machines — I’ve got a Freewrite, a 1990 Smith Corona word processor, a beautiful 1930s Underwood manual typewriter, and an impressively awful faux-vintage one from the zombie-brand era of Royal, among others — I discovered my perfect work setup is low-profile mechanicals: clicky enough to be satisfying, short enough travel to not hamper my typing speed. (I’m still slightly faster on a chiclet keyboard, but I’ll take the hit.) I spent a few years with a NuPhy Air75, but fell victim to its fairly well-known swollen battery issues, so I’ve since switched to the Iqunix MQ80 — which has less of a fun retro color scheme, but a more satisfying heft. I love the idea of giving it a cool custom keycap makeover someday, but for now I’m too cheap and lazy, and perfectly satisfied with its Mirror’s Edge-y sci-fi white. Keychrons are the equivalent of Toyotas in the mechanical keyboard world, offering reliability and a great value for the money. The 75-percent V1 Max features RGB backlighting, USB-A and USB-C 2.4GHz dongles, and modifier keycaps for both Windows and Mac. Plus, it offers simple customization that lets you remap any key to your favorite macro command. I noped out of mechanical keyboards for years because they were too clicky! No good for my shared office space. Then, Verge alum Dan Seifert clued me into the near-silent Boba U4 switches, which I installed in a Keychron V1 (love the small size, love the QMK programmable lighting, love the volume dial!) and I almost never looked back. I did have to re-spring and lube all the switches to get a familiar level of spring action, which was easy enough. Some of the switches have spontaneously stopped working over the past four years, but it only takes seconds to hot-swap another one in. And a family member bought me 8BitDo’s awesome Nintendo-themed wireless mechanical numpad, so I’m covered there too. But I don’t think it’s endgame. When I switch back from my laptop, I often miss the faster, more effortless typing I can do on its lower-profile keys. Maybe it’s time for something new. I should preface this by saying I know absolutely nothing about keyboards. My standards for what I want are pretty modest: It should work, move easily around my desk, and not hurt my wrists. Looking cool doesn’t hurt. I’ve cycled through a few over the years, mostly geometric oddities — sorry, ergonomic — like the Microsoft Sculpt Ergonomic Desktop and Logitech Ergo K860, which were rather unwieldy. I also tried ordering the Kinesis Freestyle Pro Jay uses, but gave up after the supplier tried to pass off clearly damaged kit (read: tangled hair clogging everything) as brand-new. I’ve been using the Keychron K2 Pro for a few years now. It’s compact, looks great, and holds a charge well. It has no number pad, but that keeps things nimble, and it handily switches between multiple computers I use on the go. There are also fancy lights, though I usually turn them off. The main downsides are having to regularly Google the key commands needed to control it — a pain when those lights won’t stop strobing like a hyperactive Christmas tree. It supports key remapping, so I may attempt a new layout like Dvorak sometime soon. First of all, keyboards should be clacky. I want to sound like I am firing a machine gun while I write. Second, due to assorted repetitive stress injuries, my keyboard needs to be split. So I am typing with a light-up gaming keyboard — the lights are optional, I just like them — called the Kinesis Gaming Freestyle Edge RGB. Do I game? Uh, well, I do crossword puzzles. But the Cherry MX Blue switches guarantee I sound fucking productive while I do them. Very boring Apple keyboards. Every five years the right side of the spacebar stops working and I buy a new one. MIT Technology Review
Eating disorders are on the rise. GLP-1s could make things worse.
2026-10-30

Eating disorders are on the rise. GLP-1s could make things worse.

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
GLP-1 weight-loss drugs are one of the biggest biotech breakthroughs of the last decade. But to some clinical psychologists, they can represent something more sinister—a potential trigger for eating disorders. A new report suggests that the global incidence of anorexia nervosa and bulimia nervosa shot up between 1990 and 2023—by a whopping 71%. By 2023, there were an estimated 18.1 million people living with one of these two disorders. What’s worse is that the number is likely to be much higher than that once we factor in other eating disorders. And, disturbingly, those in the field are already starting to see GLP-1 weight-loss drugs exacerbating the issue. Cheri Levinson is a clinical psychologist at the University of Louisville in Kentucky who has specialized in eating disorders for almost 20 years. She says she’s now seeing more patients with more severe symptoms than at any other time in her career, and that many of them are misusing GLP-1s. “This is the worst I’ve seen things,” she says. Eating disorders are mental health conditions in which people’s eating behaviors impact their health. They include anorexia nervosa (defined by a fear of weight gain), bulimia nervosa (in which people binge and purge), binge eating disorder, and others. It’s thought that some people have a genetic predisposition to certain eating disorders, but that some external factor is what triggers their onset. That might be stress, or social pressure to be skinny. Often, simply restricting food intake can trigger an eating disorder, says Levinson. Eating disorders—along with the pressure to look thin—were already prevalent in the 1990s (the heroin chic era springs to mind). To find out how things might have changed since then, Ruhai Bai at the Children’s Hospital of Nanjing Medical University in China and his colleagues pulled together data from studies and surveys conducted on populations around the world. The team used statistical models to fill in any data gaps. That research allowed the researchers to estimate the annual incidence of two eating disorders—anorexia nervosa and bulimia nervosa—for each of 204 countries and territories between 1990 and 2023. They also estimated the impact those disorders had on global health by calculating their disability-adjusted life years (one DALY represents a year of healthy life lost). The findings, published in JAMA Psychiatry on Wednesday, are unsettling. Between 1990 and 2023, there was a 71% increase in the incidence of those two eating disorders, the team found. Those conditions had a huge impact on global health, too. In 2023, they “cost the world the equivalent of about 3.83 million years of healthy life,” says Bai. Bai and his colleagues expect these figures to continue to rise. In 2023, an estimated 18.1 million people had either anorexia nervosa or bulimia nervosa; by 2050, the team predict, the two conditions could affect 21.8 million people. It gets worse. Those two conditions represent less than half of all eating disorders, says Levinson, who was not involved in Bai’s study. The true impact of these disorders is “probably three times the rate that they’re reporting,” she says. Bai and his colleagues didn’t explore the reasons for this rise (although Bai points out that it can’t be entirely explained by population growth). So how did we get here? Social media may have played a role. “Social media comparisons, influencers, challenges, diet trends can all be precipitating … events for an eating disorder,” says Cynthia Bulik, a clinical psychologist at the University of North Carolina, who was not involved in the new study. Bulik has been studying and treating eating disorders since the early 1980s. “Between 1990 and 2023 there has clearly been an upsurge of those types of environmental exposures,” she says. Many social media users are exposed to filtered and modified images of skinny influencers who may have undergone cosmetic surgery or extreme dieting and who are promoting “thinspiration” or “fitspiration” content. But users with eating disorders appear to be more likely to engage with this content—and to be fed more of it as a result. When researchers at the University of Melbourne investigated this phenomenon a couple of years ago, they found that the algorithms of TikTok users with eating disorders delivered 4343% more “toxic eating disorder videos” than those of users who didn’t have the disorders. They also got more content focused on dieting or exercising. Many were fed over 1000 problematic videos per month. Bulik describes social media as “quicksand” for people with, or at risk of, eating disorders. The covid-19 pandemic—and the social isolation that accompanied lockdowns—also appears to have contributed to the rise in eating disorders. Research conducted in 2021 found that the number of people hospitalized with eating disorders in the US doubled between 2018 and 2020. Researchers don’t yet have good data on how trends in eating disorders might have progressed since 2023. But anecdotally, things seem to be getting even worse. “There are more eating disorders, more severe eating disorders… and we’re seeing earlier ages of onset,” says Levinson. “We’re seeing eight- and nine-year-olds with eating disorders.” “From what I’m hearing and seeing, I am not going to be surprised if that number isn’t at least doubling again,” she adds. Levinson particularly worries about the impact of GLP-1 weight-loss drugs, which have exploded in popularity in recent years. A Gallup survey conducted earlier this year suggested that 15% of Americans had used one of these drugs for weight loss. And they’re becoming more accessible—drug companies are working on new formulations that are both easier to take and more effective, and many people can buy versions of these drugs from compound pharmacies or dubious online sources. In a small study published earlier this year, Levinson and her colleagues found that 30% of people with eating disorders had used GLP-1 drugs. The drugs might benefit some of those people. But 10% appeared to have misused the drugs. And Levinson thinks that figure is also an underestimate. Levinson says she and her colleagues have seen children develop eating disorders after being prescribed GLP-1s for medical reasons. They’ve seen others—even children as young as 11—buy versions of these drugs over TikTok. “We’re very concerned that GLPs will lead to even faster and more extreme weight loss in people with anorexia nervosa, and that patients will come to us in even more advanced stages of illness that become difficult to reverse,” says Bulik. Both Bulik and Levinson are continuing to study the impacts of these drugs in eating disorders. In the meantime, both urge medical professionals to consider the risks GLP-1s might pose to people with those disorders. Bulik contributed to a set of research and clinical guidelines, published earlier this month, that recommends doctors screen for eating disorders before prescribing the drugs, for example. The guidelines also recommend more patient education and more robust monitoring of people who take GLP-1s. If you think you or someone you know might have an eating disorder, you can find information and support at the National Eating Disorders Association. This article first appeared in The Checkup, MIT Technology Review’s weekly biotech newsletter. To receive it in your inbox every Thursday, and read articles like this first, sign up here. MIT Technology Review
We urgently need to monitor AI agents’ actions in real time
2026-10-29

We urgently need to monitor AI agents’ actions in real time

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
This summer, hundreds of OpenAI agents tried to cheat on a cybersecurity test. They broke out of their test environment and successfully hacked Hugging Face, a site that they believed held clues about how they’d be graded. Around the same time, another OpenAI agent accessed private files from an Australian government website. It’s a problem that’s by no means confined to OpenAI. In the months since, Anthropic, Google, and Meta have also disclosed incidents in which their agents have gone off track. And worryingly, in many cases the agents eluded the very researchers and safety teams tasked with monitoring them. AI agents are taking unauthorized actions, and the companies building and running them are mostly relying on luck and hindsight to mitigate any harmful impacts. They must do more to ensure safety. That’s why EU regulators and US agencies are rightfully calling for agent developers and deployers to monitor agents in real time. But these rules assume that companies can see what their agents are doing and why—when in reality, they lack sufficient visibility. As agents roll out across critical sectors, from finance to government to medicine, we must be able to monitor them in real time to prevent wide-ranging and potentially devastating consequences.  And while safety depends on monitoring, monitoring requires observability: the capacity to capture what the system did, process it into signals, and present those to humans or automated oversight systems. In other words, we need to know what the agent did and why it did it. And as our recent research shows, current agent frameworks—the software that runs agents— do not reliably generate the crucial observability records we need to ensure that agents are deployed safely. Agents present new kinds of problems that generative AI alone does not. They inherit the unpredictability of the LLMs they are built on, but where a generative AI system produces a single output for a human to act on, an agent performs longer tasks—it plans, picks tools, spawns sub-agents, and takes actions. So an agent’s course can take shape in a multitude of different ways, and no tests before launch can fully anticipate the risks. This makes observing the agents while they work even more challenging, and even more important. With colleagues from academia, civil society, and industry, we studied the four most widely used agent frameworks to see what signals they actually emit. We found major gaps that made it hard to achieve key safety goals, like noticing policy violations, unauthorized actions, and even human oversight failures. Key observability requirements were missing—like Agent IDs that persist over time, and records about which humans approved agent tasks. This made it hard to tell which agents did what or assess whether they were taking unauthorized actions. The good news is that these gaps, once identified, can be closed. Doing so starts with capturing two types of records: those that reveal what happened in an agent’s run and how the agent made its decisions. The “what happened” records identify the agent (while preserving privacy) and log how humans intervened and whether agent permissions or “memory” shifted, while the “decision” records show an agent’s reasoning and levels of confidence in the actions it’s taken. Take, for example, agents working to process a bank’s mortgage loans. The system examines an application and recommends denying it, and then a human loan officer signs off on the decision. In a later audit, or perhaps even in real time, a bank or regulator needs to be able to reconstruct which agent made the call, which officer approved it, and how long they took. And the bank must also analyze records of how the agent arrived at its refusal decision in order to ensure it did not use off-limits information, like details about the neighborhood the applicant is from. Recording “what happened” and “how the agent decided” require different approaches, but both are feasible. The current lack of “what happened” records is mostly a software plumbing problem that can be addressed through OpenTelemetry, an open observability standard maintained by engineers across the industry and by the agent frameworks adopting it. OpenTelemetry has launched a working group drafting shared formats for tracing agents, but they have yet to be finalized. The group should urgently incorporate standards for maintaining persistent agent identity and for recording human interventions and changes to agents’ permission or memory. And it should be done before these systems can scale, all the while attending to the privacy implications of agent ID that links to real people. Preserving “how agents decided” records is more challenging. The issue raises open research questions for model providers and researchers: Does the step-by-step reasoning a model displays, known as chain of thought, reflect how the model actually made a decision? And do we have reliable ways to measure how confident the agent is in that decision? These questions need answers before anyone sets standards for how such observability records are used. No single organization or sector can close the observability gaps on their own, but together, they can support the infrastructure required to prevent dangerous incidents and support safety. AI developers building agent frameworks, like OpenAI, Anthropic, CrewAI, and LangChain, must integrate telemetry signals recording an agent’s persistent identity, any human interventions, and any changes to an agent’s permissions and memory. Meanwhile, enterprises adopting these agent frameworks have an influential role to play; enterprises in regulated industries, like banks and hospitals, can put pressure on the agent developers to supply these missing observability records where standards do not yet exist. Enterprises must also define policies, permissions, and roles that set out what agents can safely do, since telemetry alone cannot assess whether agents broke specific rules. Finally, regulators and AI standards institutes must specify the observability records required for high-stakes AI deployments while leaving the technical details to industry. We have already seen initial momentum here: NIST’s agent standards initiative in the US is working on agent identity, one of the most salient gaps we found in our research. No bank would let a thousand unbadged employees loose on customer accounts and hope to piece together what happened when something goes wrong. Yet that’s how we’re deploying AI agents. Making their behavior observable is the first step toward fixing this. And by building the regulatory and policy ecosystem that makes it possible to act on what we see, we can hold organizations accountable. Until then, every task we hand to an agent hands over our agency, too. Madhulika Srikumar is the Head of the AI Safety Program at the Partnership on AI, a global non-profit partnership of leading industry, academic, and civil society organizations, advancing best practices in AI governance. Claire Leibowicz is the Director of AI, Trust, and Society at the Partnership on AI and a doctoral candidate at Oxford University. Our climate models have a big, dangerous blind spot
2026-10-29

Our climate models have a big, dangerous blind spot

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Climate change itself is increasingly fueling climate change. Rising concentrations of greenhouse gases are driving up global temperatures, setting off dangerous feedback loops that we have not fully accounted for in our climate models—or our emissions-reduction goals. Scientists have long warned that warming would propel further warming in various ways, but a growing number of observations and studies are at last providing a sharper sense of just how powerful these cascading effects could be. And, by extension, they’re showing how much faster the planet may warm in this century. A study in Environmental Research Letters in September, synthesizing the results of previous papers, found that the additional carbon dioxide and methane released from thawing permafrost, intensifying wildfires, hotter lakes and reservoirs, and wetter, warmer wetlands alone could drive global average temperatures up 0.2 °C to 0.4 °C this century. That would come on top of the 2.6°C of warming above preindustrial levels that modelers are already expecting through 2100. The additional heat could subject many of the world’s cities to monthslong heat waves and expose hundreds of millions of people to flooding. None of the models that inform the major UN climate reports, which assess how much the planet will likely warm under various emissions scenarios, account for all these feedback effects. Many of them don’t incorporate any. That leaves a huge blind spot in our understanding of how much the world could actually warm in the coming decades—and how rapidly nations will need to slash emissions to stay within critical boundaries. A separate analysis published in One Earth in March, which examined what the feedback problem means for our international climate goals, concluded that the world may be much further off track than is widely believed. It found that just three regional sources of warming-driven emissions—thawing permafrost, increasing fires in boreal forests, and warming tropical wetlands—may shorten by as much as 25% the time before the planet consistently breaches the 1.5 °C and 2 °C warming thresholds. Our climate models and the UN’s reports clearly need to be updated to better represent these effects. Nations must also step up their efforts to reduce emissions to reflect this improving understanding of the dangers we face, says Sam Abernethy, the lead author of the paper and a climate scientist at Spark Climate Solutions, a San Francisco–based nonprofit research group. At least some of this is starting to happen. Spark kick-started an initiative, called the Warming-Induced Greenhouse Gas Emissions Model Intercomparison Project, through which more than 20 international teams are running the same set of analyses on different models to develop greater understanding of feedback effects’ potential power. Researchers involved hope that the UN will incorporate those results into its next major climate assessment, due out in 2029. That report will update estimates of the remaining emissions that can be released before the world crosses key temperature thresholds. But what is already clear is that the planet is rapidly warming and that nations haven’t done nearly enough to cut fossil-fuel pollution. And even if we did manage to halt it, warming-driven emissions could continue. So a number of the research groups involved in the recent studies have started to explore what nations could do to get feedback emissions under control. Scientists at the Woodwell Climate Research Center in Massachusetts are, for example, investigating the possibility of preventing fires or rapidly detecting them and putting them out when they threaten to burn the vegetation and soil that help insulate particularly carbon-rich wedges of permafrost (a subject I explored at length in a recent feature story). In addition, the Environmental Defense Fund’s Climate Innovation Initiative has backed lab research on the potential of adding sulfate, iron, or other compounds to wetlands to counteract fast-rising methane emissions. Several startups are also exploring possibilities. They’re looking into suppressing the lightning strikes that are igniting more and more fires in high latitudes, placing synthetic mats above permafrost to prevent it from melting, and deploying autonomous drones to quickly suppress potentially high-emissions fires before they burn out of control. Investigating all these ideas is worthwhile. But researchers are only in the earliest stages of figuring out how big a dent in emissions any such efforts could achieve across sprawling global ecosystems undergoing massive transformations. The surest path to reducing the effect of warming-driven warming remains the one we’ve known all along: breaking society’s addiction to fossil fuels. This article is from The Spark, MIT Technology Review’s weekly climate newsletter. To receive it in your inbox every Wednesday, sign up here. The newly public company is betting that AI’s power boom will help turn enhanced geothermal energy into a scalable reality. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. MIT Technology Review
Creating AI value with enterprise context
2026-10-28

Creating AI value with enterprise context

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Across a business, any number of AI platforms, systems, and tools may be at work simultaneously—for example, AI-powered customer platforms, conversational assistants, and workflow and process automation tools. Each one is sophisticated, advanced, and quick to respond. But, when set to work, the outcomes and actions that emerge may be wrong, confused, or lacking a proper understanding of the business. Many organizations find themselves in this situation—and it could be one of key reasons they struggle to extract returns on their AI investments. An MIT Technology Review Insights poll finds that while every organization surveyed is using AI today to some degree, fewer than one in five (16%) are extracting transformative value from their deployments. The largest percentage of respondents (40%) say they are seeing moderate gains in specific tasks or teams, while 21% report meaningful gains, leaving 23% that are seeing minimal returns or none at all. Companies are mostly still piloting AI use cases (according to 38% of respondents) or using it to assist employees’ work with tools like co-pilots or chat assistants (35%). Just 12% of enterprises have AI agents in production and 3% say they are using AI to do most of the work in key processes. “There’s a big gap between what the models are capable of and how the models are actually being useful inside organizations,” says Prukalpa Sankar, co-founder of Atlan. “For every real-life intent and purpose, artificial general intelligence is already here. It’s just not really useful in most companies today.” The missing layer, Sankar says, is context, or how well AI understands a business. A context layer is the architectural infrastructure that sits between an organization’s business systems (for example, systems of record and data, and its AI platforms). It translates enterprise intelligence into governed, machine-usable context for AI agents or models and helps knowledge compound. “Without a context layer for AI, it’s almost like you’re hiring the smartest intern in the world, blindfolding them, and throwing them into your organization,” Sankar says. This content was produced by Insights, MIT Technology Review’s custom content arm, not its editorial staff. It was researched and written by humans, with any AI tools that may have been used limited to production processes under human oversight. Game developers have been hitting refusals from Claude Code since late October, some on projects they say they have a right to build. Bruno Ferreira reported the complaints for Tom’s Hardware on Saturday, citing a wave of issues on Claude Code’s public GitHub tracker. TNW read the issues. Anthropic has not announced a change, and its Claude Code changelog does not mention one. Tom’s Hardware said there was no official statement. The owner of Conflict Simulations LLC builds personalised digital versions of board wargames for clients who own the games. Around 27 October, Claude Code began getting “a server-side instruction” to refuse game work, the developer wrote in a GitHub issue on 30 October. Sessions now refuse the work, citing an instruction against reproducing games. “A blanket category rule against ‘games’ cannot tell the difference between piracy and commissioned professional work for a game’s owner.” The owner of Conflict Simulations LLC, on GitHub In an update, the developer said the sessions kept doing the work after the rule reportedly arrived. The refusals began only after they asked Claude to check whether the rule affected their projects. They continued in new sessions on the same code. One commenter suggested the project’s memory or Git history now carried the refusal, and the developer said they would try to clear it. Anthropic’s support bot told the developer the policy covers all customers, with no exception, the developer wrote. Both support tickets were later passed to staff, and the developer has filed an appeal. They asked Anthropic to aim the rule at real copyright infringement and to offer a verification path for professionals. Not everyone on the thread agreed. One commenter said that unless the work is for the rights holders, it is no different from any other reverse-engineered port. Other developers reported a separate kind of block this month. Claude Code’s cyber safeguards flagged anti-cheat code for one developer’s game and a security review of another’s own game. Tom’s Hardware said the timing of the two kinds of restriction may be a coincidence. Anthropic says its generally available models have conservative cyber safeguards that block most cyber work. On 6 October it expanded its Cyber Verification Program, which lifts some of those blocks for vetted security teams. Members of Project Glasswing, its earlier programme for its Mythos model, move into the top tier. The company also faces copyright claims, including a suit over 274 Elvis Presley songs filed in October. Separately, a tampered Claude Code extension reached Cursor users on 29 October. French MPs vote for a €0.055 tax on every gigabyte sent to users

French MPs vote for a €0.055 tax on every gigabyte sent to users

The Next Web | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
The chamber of France’s National Assembly at the Palais Bourbon in Paris, September 2024. France’s National Assembly has voted to tax companies for every gigabyte of data they send to French internet users. The measure, an amendment to the 2027 budget, would charge content providers €0.055 per gigabyte. Its Socialist authors estimate it would raise €10bn a year. It passed by 11 votes to 10 on Friday, with 14 abstentions, according to the official record of the session. The government and the finance committee both opposed it. The vote is only a first step. The tax must survive the rest of the budget process to become law. Internet providers would measure the data flowing to their customers and note which company sent it, under the text of the amendment. They would report the totals to the tax authorities every quarter, under the supervision of telecoms regulator Arcep. The text bars any record of what the data contains or where it goes. Some data travels through content delivery networks, which store copies close to users. That data would count as it leaves the network. The text caps the tax at 15% of a company’s French revenue or 2% of its global revenue. The amendment’s explanatory note cites an Arcep estimate that 200bn gigabytes reach French internet subscribers each year. It says Google, Meta, Amazon, Netflix and Akamai account for about half of that traffic. “I fail to see how a tax can be passed on to users of Facebook, Instagram or TikTok, since these services are free.” Laurent Lhardit, the amendment’s author, in the Assembly (TNW translation) Public accounts minister David Amiel told MPs the scheme would need sensors on the networks, or measurement on users’ own devices. Half of the tax would fall on smaller content providers, many of them French, he said. On the authors’ own estimate, that is about €5bn. Philippe Juvin, the finance committee’s general rapporteur, warned that the big platforms could pass the cost on to advertisers. VideoLAN, the French nonprofit behind the VLC media player, said the tax would cost it more than €1m a year. VLC has passed 6 billion downloads. “So, the French socialists, and Parliament members just voted a tax, that they don’t understand, which would cost VideoLAN more than 1 million euro per year.” VideoLAN, on X The Senate, where the conservative Les Républicains hold a majority, is unlikely to back it, Martin Clavey reported for Next. The government could also force the budget through without a final vote. In that case, it can leave the amendment out of the text, Next noted. In June, President Trump threatened 100% tariffs over digital services taxes. Amiel told MPs France already has tools to tax big tech, such as its digital services tax. He also pointed to EU competition fines, which France says should cut EU contributions. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! EU tells countries to pause national social media bans for a year

The Next Web | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
The European Parliament’s plenary chamber in Strasbourg. The European Commission has told EU countries to put national social media bans for children on hold for 12 months. It wants a single EU law instead. Austria is the first country affected, and its plan to keep under-14s off social media is now frozen. Commission spokesperson Thomas Regnier announced the pause in Brussels on Thursday, as Eliza Gkritsi reported for Politico Europe. AFP also reported the decision. “The Commission has decided to postpone for 12 months any draft national laws overlapping with the future harmonised rules.” Thomas Regnier, Commission spokesperson, to reporters “It is unacceptable for Brussels to block member states from enacting national laws without presenting a completed European solution of its own.” Alexander Pröll, Austrian state secretary, to Politico Europe EU countries must send draft technical laws to Brussels before they adopt them. Austria sent its draft on 28 July, according to the Commission’s notification database. It sets an age limit of 14 for video-sharing platforms and requires age checks. The Commission’s review period ended on Thursday. Vice-Chancellor Andreas Babler told Politico Europe he had “no sympathy” for the delay. Vienna will keep preparing its law so the ban can start as soon as the pause ends, he said. Other countries are in a different position. Greece passed its under-15 ban last week. Athens sent its law to Brussels before the EU proposal, so it will likely keep it, Politico Europe reported. The Commission has asked France to rework its draft but is not expected to block it, AFP reported, because Paris also notified early. France’s Constitutional Council struck down the first French ban in August. Drafts from other countries, including Italy, are still under review. The Commission proposed the EU Kids Act in September. It would bar under-13s from most social media and limit access for under-15s. Brussels wants one standard so platforms do not face 27 different age limits. It also fears countries will lose interest in the EU law once their own rules are in place, AFP reported. The European Parliament and EU governments still need to agree on the Kids Act. Politico Europe reported on Tuesday that it may not take effect until 2028. Several capitals do not want to wait. Denmark is pushing ahead with its own law, Politico Europe reported. “The problems are urgent,” Danish digital minister Christina Egelund told the outlet. Meta built a humanoid robot with China’s Fourier, The Information reports
2026-11-01

Meta built a humanoid robot with China’s Fourier, The Information reports

The Next Web | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Fourier’s GR-3 humanoid robot, which the company unveiled in August 2025. Meta has built a prototype humanoid robot with Chinese robotics company Fourier in about three months, and the two are already working on a second version. Jyoti Mann reported the project for The Information on Saturday, citing people familiar with it. The robot is known inside Meta as Max and weighs about 48kg (106 pounds). Meta told The Information that the work with Fourier is for internal research and experimentation, and that it has no plans to sell the units. TNW has not independently verified the report. Max stands about 1.5 metres tall and runs on Nvidia’s Jetson Thor chip, according to The Information. It has a screen that mimics facial expressions, built-in speakers and five-fingered hands for picking up small objects. Meta’s robotics team travelled to China to work with Fourier. Li-Chen Miller, Meta’s vice president of product for robotics, spent almost two months at Fourier’s factory in Shanghai, the report said. Meta then showed Max to some staff at an internal robotics summit in late August. Work on Max 2 began in September. It is due to get a new torso, better hands, a projector and extra sensors to help it handle stairs. Meta sees humanoids as a physical form for Muse, its AI agent, and hopes they can do household tasks, The Information reported. Nvidia, whose chip runs Max, agreed this week to invest $3bn in Meta and expand their work on Muse. Meta has been building up its robotics team. In May it bought Assured Robot Intelligence, a San Diego start-up that makes AI software for robots. Its team joined Meta Superintelligence Labs, home to the group that leads Meta’s humanoid work, Maria Deutscher reported for SiliconANGLE. Fourier is based in Shanghai. In August 2025 it unveiled GR-3, a 165cm humanoid it calls a “care-bot”. It is built as a companion, for example for older adults. Meta joins a long list of companies chasing home and factory humanoids. Tesla has started building Optimus robots, and Agility’s Digit 5 humanoid will run on Nvidia hardware. In China, rival maker Unitree is working on AI that lets its robots improve themselves. Meta has not set a launch date for a humanoid product, one of The Information’s sources said.
